Packers Seek Consistency 


By Art Daley 
Green Bay Packer Yearbook Editor 


The Packers were excellent, good, 
bad and pretty bad at times during the 
1980 season. 

They did all sorts of unbelievable 
things—like beating the Bears 12-6 in 
overtime on a missed field goal in 
Game 1, permitting the same Bears 61 


Coach Bart Starr claps and yells encouragement from sideline. 


2 


points in Game 14, beating the 
Division Champion Vikings twice, 
losing heartbreakers to powerhouses 
Cleveland and Pittsburgh, etc. 

The Packers produced yards at a 
record pace...but, alas, they just 
couldn't turn them into enough points. 
They scored under 20 points in 10 of 
the 16 games. 

Defensively, the Packers allowed 


under 20 points in only five games, 
including the commendable totals of 6, 
9 and 3 against the Bears, Bengals and 
Vikings. 

It all added up to a record of 5 wins, 
10 losses and 1 tie. Three of the wins 
came in Green Bay, one in Milwaukee 
and one in Minnesota. The tie was 
played in Tampa Bay. 

The past season got off to a most 
devastating start when both starting 
quarterbacks, Lynn Dickey and David 
Whitehurst, were kayoed for the pre- 
season in the Hall of Fame opener in 
Canton, Ohio. Then the defense took 
its losses—Rich Wingo, Mike Hunt 
and later John Anderson via injury. 

The offense was set back and didn’t 
recover until the fifth league game. 
And the defense, which was 
introducing the 3-4 for the first time, 
suffered a few growing pains—not to 
mention loss of three linebackers. 

The obvious problem in 1980 was 
inconsistency—in scoring, defensive 
play and, seemingly at times, in 
enthusiasm. 

Coach Bart Starr feels “we've got to 
be more consistent. That’s going to be 
our byword in 1981—consistency.” 

And now comes the 1981 season— 
Green Bay's 62nd in the NFL. 

How can the Packers reverse the 
record to, say, 10-5-1. 

Let’s look at the offense. No. 1 goal: 
More points. 

The offensive line will get special 
attention. If Mark Koncar makes a 
complete recovery from an achilles 
heel surgery, the line will get 
exceptional help on the left side. To 
make things interesting, there are 
three good prospects from last year, 
Karl Swanke, Syd Kitson and Buddy 
Aydelette, who will push veterans 
Greg Koch, Leotis Harris and Derrel 
Gofourth. 

The line, middled by talented and 
hard-working Larry McCarren, is the 
key to the offense. 

Starr is hopeful that the Packers 
can boost their rushing yardage from 
3.4 in 1980 to just over 4.0. Eddie Lee 


Ivery and Gerry Ellis, who had so 
many bright moments last year, are 
the key figures. If they can run 
effectively, Lynn Dickey’s passing will 
be considerably more valuable. 

Dickey broke seven Packer passing 
records last year, an amazing feat 
when you consider the rushing game 
was not a big threat. James Lofton is 
the receiving key, of course, but he 
badly needs somebody to help remove 
some of the double teaming he 
generally receives. 


The Packers drafted a big and fast 
tight end in Gary Lewis. He also is 
swift enough to play wide receiver. 
Lewis along with Paul Coffman could 
give the Packers a new two-tight end 
offense to help take some of the 
pressure off Lofton. Sophomore Fred 
Nixon also figures to blossom into a 
premier wide receiver. 

Dickey had his best season in pro 
football in 1980, escaping any of the 
injuries that plagued him in previous 
league seasons. And he’s looking 
forward to an even better 1981. The 
QB spot is getting special scrutiny this 
year due to the presence of Rich 
Campbell, the California star who was 
the Pack’s No. 1 draft choice. 

The 1980 offense scored 231 points, 
an average of 14.4, and produced 
5,097 yards, an average of 318 yards 
per game. But to show the 
inconsistency, the offense averaged 
228 yards per game in the first four 
contests, 391 the next eight games and 
262 the last four. 

There was a spell in mid-season 
when the Packers’ offense led the 
entire league in yardage production. 

Defensively, the Packers are bound 
to improve considerably because they 
will know the intracacies of the 3-4 
defense better than they did a year 
ago. The 3-4 became the basic defense 
but Defense Coordinator John Meyer 
introduced a whole batch of new looks 
to go with it. These should be second 
nature in ’81, 

The heart of the defense unit is 
linebacker and this is where the 
Packers hope to improve the most 
with the return of Wingo, Anderson 
and George Cumby to go with the 
hard-hitting and active Mike 
Douglass. To provide more 
competition, an additional eight 
linebackers will compete, including 
three draft choices and holdovers Ed 
O'Neil, Kurt Allerman, Jim Gueno 
and Paul Rudzinski. 

The defensive line is pretty well 
intact with Ezra Johnson and Mike 


That touchdown feeling. James Lofton enjoys as Paul Coffman prepares to 
spike ball after scoring TD vs. Tampa Bay in Milwaukee. 


Butler at the ends and Charley 
Johnson at nose tackle, but they'll be 
pressed hard by such holdovers as 
Casey Merrill, Terry Jones, and Troy 
Thomas and draftees Rich Turner, a 
good nose tackle from Oklahoma, and 
Byron Braggs of Alabama. 

The defensive backfield may 
undergo some changes, with Mark 
Lee and Mike Jolly possibly seeing 
more action. Johnnie Gray is an 
important fixture who may be 
switched from free safety to strong 
safety. Gray made five of the team’s 13 
interceptions last year while non- 
starters Jolly and Wylie Turner each 
snared two. 

Steve Luke, Estus Hood and Mike 
McCoy will return to fight for starting 
status, along with Mark Murphy and 
draftee Bill Whitaker. 

The new rule allowing more 
freedom for passers and receivers 
started to take hold in 1980 and most 
teams started to revamp their 


defensive backfields. The Packers 
likely will look at more combinations 
this year. 

The Packers’ special teams play 
could be improved considerably, with 
experts in their field manning the 
punting and place kicking jobs. 

The Pack drafted a punter on the 
third round, one Ray Stachowicz of 
Michigan State. Stachowicz averaged 
43.3 yards during his college career in 
all sorts of weather—the kind he will 
encounter in Green Bay. 

The Packers are looking at four 
kickers headed by the talented Jan 
Stenerud, who came to the Pack’s 
rescue late in 1980. Jan, the former 
Kansas City star, is starting his 15th 
year. He’ll be challenged by Don 
Sweet, onetime star in Canada, Skip 
Vernon and Rade Savich. 

And there you are...a briefing on 
the 1981 Packers. Let’s all hope fora 
lot of consistency and a lack of 
injuries.

Money and the 


By Art Daley 
Green Bay Packer Yearbook Editor 


These two pages have a greenish 
tinge. 

The reason is that the article 
contained therein is about money .. 
Packer football money. 

People don’t pay money to see the 
Packers toss money around. They pay 
to see them toss the football around. 

Thus, if they play football well 
people become more excited about 
the Packers and thus become more 
anxious to see them play football. 

Those few paragraphs sound like a 
first grade reader but they are so 
constructed for the purpose of 
introducing the off-beat subject 
known as money. 

Money has been an important part 
of the Packers since the word go—the 
price of the franchise ($250), haggling 
with players over salaries (like $40 or 
$50 a game), formation of the Packer 
corporation to form a sound financial 
base, the sale of non-profit stock to 
keep the club alive, the first TV 
income. It could go on and on. 

Money is a mild springtime subject 
in Packerville because that’s when (1) 
the Packers hold their annual 
stockholders’ meeting and (2) the 
signing of new draft choices starts. 


Packers 


The stockholder session is most 
unique because it’s the only one of its 
kind held in the National Football 
League. 

You see, these stockholders don’t 
make any money. 

The Packers have 1,757 
stockholders and they own 4,624) 
shares of non-profit stock. The bulk of 
the stockholders are those folks who 
bought $25 shares of stock in the 
Packers in the winter of 1949-50. 

More than $150,000 was raised, the 
club was saved and seven years later 
(1957) the City of Green Bay built the 
Packers a new stadium seating over 
32,000. The Packers, themselves, built 
the additions to the stadium and now 
the park seats over 56,000, 

The 1981 stockholders’ meeting 
was held in downtown Green Bay on 
Monday night, May 4 with an 
attendance of more than 100 
stockholders’ plus 39 of the club’s 45 
directors. 

The meeting lasted about 65 
minutes and it was peaceful despite 
the club’s losing record. President 
Dominic Olejniczak ran the meeting 
and the main features were the 
corporation's annual financial report 
and some comments by Coach Bart 
Starr on the team. 


A profit of $1,298,010 was 


announced on 1980 operations in a 
profit and loss statement presented to 
each stockholder. This is the third 
highest gain in the club's history. They 
had $1.8 million profit in 1979 and $1.5 
in 1978. 

The Packers’ financial figures give 
you an idea what other owners are 
pocketing out of their operations, 
although these figures would vary by 
virtue of stadium size and reaching the 
playoffs. 

The Packers were one of the higher 
paying clubs in the 1960s because the 
players were producing champion- 
ships. Despite the lack of success on 
the field in the 1970s, Green Bay has 
still maintained a good, solid 
reputation for paying well (and still 
keeping a reasonable salary structure) 
despite the jump of Bruce Clark to 
Canada in 1980. This could have been 
an agent-prompted flight and already 
Clark is unhappy in Canada. Clark is 
only the third first choice the Pack lost 
to Canada in 45 years of drafting. Not 
the worst record of paying first picks. 

The profit figures for 1980 sounded 
rosy, of course, but there was a word 
of warning at the 1981 meeting. 

The income from actual football 
operations in 1980 was $739,482. The 
1979 football income was $1,981,107. 
Thus, 1980's income was $1,241,625 
down from the previous year. The big 
jump came in player salaries (season 
expense), training camp and playing 
one extra pre-season game (Canton 
Hall of Fame). 

The bulk of the income for 1980 
came from interest on investments 
from their surplus. This interest, 
largely in solid investments such as 
treasury bills and municipals, totaled 
$1,257,054. 

Green Bay has a most unique 
structure. The Packers’ original 
charter made them a non-profit 
corporation. Thus, they are able to 
accumulate dollars and their surplus 
as of the end of 1980 was listed at 
$18,320,123. Of that amount, 
approximately $14 million can be 
invested. The remainder is in fixed 
assets. 

Other clubs in the NFL can’t pile up 
dollars in this way and the income at 
the end of the year, in some cases, 
supports several families. In other 
cases, the franchise might bea branch 
ofa larger business, a partnership or a 
company. 

The non-profit charter under which 
the corporation still operates was set 
up in the early 1920s by a group of

businessmen headed by Andrew 
Turnbull, the club's first president. 
They provided that if the corporation 
folded the proceeds would go to the 
local American Legion post. 

The founders also provided in the 
corporation’s by-laws that no 
stockholder shall ever receive a return 
on his investment either through 
dividends or distribution of assets. 

This is the real secret to why the 
Packers are the only team in the NFL. 
Operating on its original franchise. It is 
a practical impossibility for the Packer 
franchise ever to be sold or 
transferred. 

Franchises in the NFL are 
irrevocable; that is, the league cannot 
force their sale or termination even if 


Dominic Olejniczak

so. 

So the only way the Packer 
franchise could ever be sold or 
terminated would be by the voluntary 
action of two-thirds of its 
stockholders. 

Can you imagine two-thirds of the 
Pack’s rabid shareholding hometown 
fans ever voting to give up their 
franchise? 

The Packers’ success, like most 
other clubs in the NFL, rests quite a 
bit on the shoulders of the athletes on 
the field. If they win, the problems 
diminish and often become 
inconsequential. If the team loses, the 
problems increase and the natives 
become restless. 

The club has restructured its 
organization to the extent that Bart 
Starr has been relievec of his general 
manager's duties so that he can 
devote fulltime to football operations. 
Olejniczak, elected to a 24th 
consecutive term as president, also 
becomes chief executive officer. He 
will work directly with Starr and 
Corporate Assistants Tom Miller and 
Bob Harlan. 

And there you are ... Money and 
the Green Bay Packers! 

P.S. if you hold a stock certificate 
from that 1950 stock sale each $25 
share now would be worth $3,961.64 

.. IF you could cash it in.

You Have To 
Get Better 
Every Year 


— Gerry Ellis 


By Bob McGinn 
Green Bay Press-Gazette 


By most standards, the Packer 
locker room is a commodious, 
comfortable place for players to call 
home. 

The individual lockers, identified by 
green and gold name cards above 
each stall, are well spaced. It may not 
compare with an easy chair in front of 
a fireplace, but for a locker room, it 
gets the job done. 

There is a trouble spot, though, in 
one corner where a door leads to the 
practice field. It is much-traveled. In 
fact, it can be a downright bottleneck 
minutes after workouts. 

It is there, with the absolute worst 
locker location in the room, that 
Gerry Ellis found a home in the 
National Football League. 

Ellis, a victim of the Los Angeles 
Rams’ final cut a week earlier, got an 
SOS call from the Packers and signed 
as a free agent Sept. 10. 

Four days later he played against 
the Detroit Lions. Eleven days later he 
was the starting fullback against none 
other than the Rams. About two 
months later he earned the right to be 
called the Packers’ fullback of the 
future. 

It was heady wine for Ellis, a 
confident young man who always felt 
he was better than a lot of other 
people did. Heading the list of 
doubters was Rams’ coach Ray 
Malavasi. Heading the list of semi- 
doubters was Warren Powers, Ellis’ 
coach at the University of Missouri. 


6 


Gerry Ellis evades 49er after block by Eddie Lee Ivery in Packer victory in 
Milwaukee. 


Then Ellis’ path crossed with Bart 
Starr, a coach in dire need of hungry 
football players. 

“IT got up here and Bart asked me if| 
wanted to play,” said Ellis. “I told him, 
‘Yeah, | want to play.’ He’s the one 
who gave me a chance to play, andit’s 
been pretty good ever since.” 

Indeed. Ellis was a tough, 
swashbuckling, second-effort kind of 
runner. He had a low center of gravity 
and was tough to knock down. His 
pass-catching ability was impressive. 
He had sure hands and he made the 
difficult catches. 

Of the Packers’ top draft choices in 
1980, Bruce Clark wound up in 


Canada, George Cumby twice 
underwent knee surgery and Mark 
Lee failed to crack the starting lineup. 

So, when it came time to select the 
team’s rookie of the year, it was a 
foregone conclusion that the award 
would go to Ellis. It did, but it should 
not be inferred that it was undeserved. 

In fact, Ellis’ rushing total of 545 
yards was the second best by a first- 
year Packer in the 62-year history of 
the franchise. Only John Brockington, 
who amassed a then NFL-rookie 
record 1,105 yards in 1971, gained 
more. 

He also caught 48 passes for 
another 496 yards and scored eight 


touchdowns, making him the team 
scoring leader. His 10.3 average per 
catch was surpassed only by Billy 
Sims of the Detroit Lions and Bruce 
Harper of the New York Jets among 
leading NFL running backs. 

Zeke Bratkowski, the Packers’ 
backfield coach, thinks Ellis had an 
outstanding season. “But the key to 
Gerry Ellis is the potential for his 
entire career,” said Bratkowski. 
“Having been put into the situation he 
was with no training camp, and 
adapting to our system, and being so 
productive. I think his career is all 
ahead of him.” 

Ellis fervently hopes that is the case. 

“I proved that I can play to a lot of 
people,” he said. “I proved that I want 
to play. Whenever the time comes, I'll 
always be ready to play. 

“(But) you can’t go on what 
happened last year,” said Ellis. “You 
have to look ahead. I felt pretty good 
just being in the NFL. 

“Now, every year, you just have to 
get better. There’s good people 
coming out every year. You just have 
to do well to stay here.” 

Like every self-respecting back, 
Ellis wants to carry the ball. The more 
the better, he says. 

Steve Atkins and Ellis split time at 
fullback for the first half of the season, 
before Atkins suffered a season- 
ending ankle injury against Pittsburgh 
in the ninth game. 

In his first eight games, Ellis gained a 
modest 208 yards for a 3.9 average in 
6 carries per game. 

With Atkins out, Ellis went on a 
tear. He went 17-for-83 against San 
Francisco, 11-for-69 against the New 
York Giants and 15-for-101 against 
Minnesota. He averaged 14 carries, 
gained 253 yards, picked up nearly six 
yards an attempt and scored four 
touchdowns. 

Things were different after that. In 
the last four games, all defeats, Ellis 
rushed 30 times for 84 yards, a meager 
2.8 average. 

Ellis insists he did not do anything 
differently late in the year. “I went out 
there every week and did the same 
thing,” he said. 

Bratkowski put the blame on the 
team’s failures down the stretch. 

“The last four games was like a total 
team thing,” he said. “Sometimes the 
game contributes to statistics, 
because it gets off rhythm and you 
have to depend on the pass more so.” 


Continued on Page 8 


Eddie Lee Ivery, shown here escaping from the Vikings’ Jim White, had one of 
the lowest fumble ratios in the NFL in 1980, losing the ball only three times in 
202 carries and 50 pass receptions. He led the Packers in rushing with 831 
yards, averaging 4.1 yards, and added 481 yards on pass catches. 


7 


Continued from Page 7 


Ellis, he stressed, did not reach a 
plateau in his development. 

Bratkowski left little doubt as to 
who will be the starting fullback in 
1981. “He (Ellis) is going to line up right 
behind center. Atkins is going to be 
right behind him,” he said. 

Forgive Ellis if he has to pinch 
himself at such reports. Just last 
summer his professional football 
career seemed at an end before it ever 
had a beginning. 

The Rams used three of their first 
eight draft picks in 1980 to select 
running backs. Jewerl Thomas of San 
Jose State went on the third round, 
Mike Guman of Penn State went on 
the sixth round, and Ellis went on the 
seventh round. 

Two roster berths opened when 
starting halfback Wendell Tyler 
suffered a serious hip injury before 
training camp and veteran Jim Jodat 
was traded to Seattle during the 
exhibition season. The other veterans 
were Cullen Bryant, Elvis Peacock 
and Eddie Hill. Ellis liked the West 
Coast—“I mean I didn’t see a drop of 
rain and you'd just be amazed at all the 
pretty girls”—and he liked his chances 
of making the roster. 

“I didn’t make many mistakes at all, 
and | could play both positions,” he 
said, his temper still stirred toa degree 
by the Rams’ decision to waive him. 
“(But) [ think they were going with 
experience, and then in order of the 
draft. If you were drafted higher, then 
naturally you were going to stay.” 

Thomas was “legitimate,” in Ellis’ 
opinion, but he questioned why the 
Rams kept Guman and the oft-injured 
Peacock. 

“J just knew that a couple of backs 
were going to get hurt, like Eddie Hill 
and Elvis (they did),” he said. “Elvis, he 
hadn't played a full season, Eddie Hill, 
(he) wasn’t very big and I just knew | 
had him beat. 

“They had this drill set up with a 
dummy set up behind us, and the 
linebacker had to come get the 
dummy. Well, I don’t think one 
linebacker got by me. I think I fared 
better than the rest of the backs. Then 
(Bob) Brudzinski comes in, he runs 
right over Guman, | mean just run 
right over him. 

“Ijust hoped they'd cut one of those 
guys and bring in somebody fresh.” 

Ellis was right. Neither Peacock nor 
Hill survived the season. 

The end for Ellis came on final 
cutdown day, al about the same time 


8 


four veteran Ram holdouts returned 
to the team. But Ellis sensed The Turk 
would be paying him a call before that 
after he fumbled one day in practice 

“Ray Malavasi, he gets all fired up 
and hyper about me,” said Ellis. “Two 
or three plays later Jewer! Thomas 
gets upandhe fumbles three timesina 
row and Ray didn’t say a word. | just 
felt then, ‘Oh well, I guess I’m the next 
to go.’” 

After being waived, Ellis was 
contacted by San Diego, Oakland, 
New Orleans and Green Bay. He 
picked the Chargers and worked out 
with the team, but they decided not to 
proffer a contract. Oakland was next 
in line, but the Packers called and their 
situation seemed more urgent. 

The Packer staff worked him out 
and it appeared encouraging, but Ellis 
found it difficult to conceal his 
pessimism. 

“I said, ‘Well, you get cut once, you 
get cut twice, and nobody really 


Terdell Middleton 


knows what you can do.”" he recalled 
of his feelings. “They don't know if 
you're competent enough, able to play 
or can comprehend what’s going on 
out there.” 

After the brief workout, Bratkowski 
was quoted as telling Starr, “We can’t 
let an athlete of his quality get out of 
town.” 

The Packers didn’t, and it proved to 
be a wise decision. 

Bratkowski believes Ellis has 
something resembling a sixth sense on 
a football field 

“He’s a very instinctive football 
player. That’s one of his keys,” says 
Bratkowski. “He makes the 
adjustment immediately. He bounces 
off tacklers and runs back to daylight.” 

A case in point, says Bratkowski, 
was the Pittsburgh game, when Ellis 
made a shoestring catch ona broken 
pass play and turned it into a 69-yard 
touchdown, 

“He reacted to the situation, Lynn 


(Dickey) saw him, he caught the ball 
down around his shoestrings and he 
picked it up and ran for a touchdown,” 
said Bratkowski. “This is the 
instinctive athletic ability, that’s what 
it is.” 

Ellis agrees. He is a natural. He has 
that rare blend of speed, strength, 
agility and athletic sense. 

At Hickman High School in 
Columbia, Mo., where he grew up, the 
5-feet-11%, 220-pound Ellis threw the 
discus a state-record 188-feet-3 and 
the shot put over 60-feet, and he ran 
the 100-yard dash in 10.0. He was also 
an all-state football player and a 
starter on the basketball team. 

Ellis accomplished those things 
despite the fact that he underwent 
back surgery for a slipped disk as a 
high school freshman. The operation 
was a success and today seldom gives 
him trouble, although he admits that 
fears of a possible second disk injury 
are with him in certain blocking 
situations. 

From Hickman, Ellis attended Fort 
Scott (Kan.) Junior College for a year, 
then went back home asa sophomore 
to the University of Missouri. His 
career there was a good one but he 
was overshadowed by both James 
Wilder and Earl Gant as a junior and 
by Wilder asa senior. Ellis chafed toa 
degree, particularly early in his final 
season. 

Wilder, a second-round draft choice 
of Tampa Bay in 1981, ended the 1978 
season with an 18l-yard, four- 
touchdown burst in an upset of 
Nebraska. It signaled a campaign by 
Tiger publicists to bring him national 
recognition. 

Ellis, coming off a strong season of 
his own as the principal backup 
behind Wilder and Gant, another NFL 
draftee, remembers being somewhat 
miffed. 


Then during summer drills Wilder 
pulled a hamstring muscle and missed 
virtually all of the first three games, 
leaving Ellis as the featured back for 
the only time in his college career. He 
made the most of it, including a 173- 
yard, two-touchdown performance in 
a 146 victory over Illinois. 

Bob Bratkowski, son of the Packer 
assistant and then a part-time coach at 
Missouri, remembers the situation 
well. 

“Wilder was the guy who got the 
pub—he was considered the running 
back,” said Bratkowski, now full-time 
wide receiver coach at Weber State. 
“The latter part of Ellis’ junior year 


Wilder was phenomenal, and they 
built him up. 

“Well, Gary’s senior year Wilder 
was hurt the first three games and Ellis 
absolutely carried us, catching it and 
running. He was very responsible for 
us winning our first three games.” 

In the first three games Ellis rushed 
47 times for 270 yards, a 5.7 average. 
With Wilder’s return, Ellis went 74-for- 
314 the rest of the way, a 4.2 average. 

After one pro season, Ellis says 
bluntly that he has no physical 
limitations that would stop him from 
becoming a Pro Bow! player. All he 
worries about is getting a chance to 
showcase his talent. 

“It will take more than six carries, 
five carries, 10 carries,” he said, 
glancing at a game-by-game 
breakdown of his rookie year. “A lot of 
guys, they’re averaging 20 some 
carries a game. 

“(But) I'm not really looking for 
fame. | like to go out here and do what 
I do best. That’s play ball. 

“T guess if you do good recognition 
will come, the stardom will come if 
you're doing good enough. It’s just 
goals you set for yourself. 

“In high school, I said before I'd 
leave I'd hit 60-feet in the shot put and 
break the state discus record. Those 
things did happen. 

“Now if I say that one of these years 
T'm going to have 1,000 yards, one of 
these years I will have one.” 

As a means to such an end, Zeke 
Bratkowski wants Ellis to fumble less 
and block better. 

Last season, Ellis fumbled seven 
times, in 126 carries, giving him one of 
the highest fumbles per carry ratios in 
the NFL. Most of them, according to 
Bratkowski, came when Ellis was 
running to the right. It is his natural 
style to carry the ball in his left arm and 
hand, and when he went right 
pursuing defenders used the ball asan 
inviting target for their shoulders and 
helmets. 

“We've got to get him to change the 
ball when he gets to the point of 
contact,” said Bratkowski. 

His blocking should improve, says 
Bratkowski, with slightly better effort 
and experience 

The Packers’ other starting running 
back, Eddie Lee Ivery, had one of the 
NFL's lowest fumble ratios. He lost 
the football just three times in 202 
carries and 50 pass receptions. 

But Ivery did more than merely 
hang onto the ball. After undergoing 
severe knee surgery after his first

“You're going to see a better Eddie 
Lee Ivery than you saw last year,” said

While Ivery appeared to lose some 
of his first-step speed and shiftiness in 
the open field, Bratkowski believes he 
is a more effective player now than 
ever. 

The other key backs heading into 
training camp figure to be Atkins, the 
third-year fullback who has_ not 


finished a season, and halfback Terdell 
Middleton. Harlan Huckleby, Eric 
Torkelson and Vickey Ray Anderson 
are also veteran holdovers seeking 
roster spots. 

Ellis has something else on his mind. 

To put it simply, he wants out—of 
the corner. 

“It was nice just being in that part of 
the locker room (last year),” he 
chuckled. “But I'll try to get out of 
there this year. They can give that 
spot to somebody else who hasn't 
been here before.”

NO KICKING SURPRISES... Picase 


By Tony Walter 
Green Bay Press-Gazette 


When Dan Devine drafted Chester 
Marcol out of Hillsdale College in the 
second round of the 1972 draft, he did 
it for one reason. 

He didn’t want any more surprises. 

Devine wanted just one place- 
kicker (instead of the three he used 
during his first season in Green Bay) 
and he wanted one who would be 
around for a long time. 

It worked, of course. In fact, Marcol 
stayed in Green Bay twice as long as 
Devine and, prior to his sudden 
departure last season, climbed to the 
top of the scoring list among kickers in 
Packer history. 

When Bart Starr claimed free agent 
punter David Beverly midway through 


10 


the 1975 season, he did it for one 
reason. 

Starr 
surprises. 

It was in his first game as head 
coach in which Starr was forced to 
watch the Detroit Lions block two 
Steve Broussard punts. 

Starr wanted a punter he could 
count on to get the ball away in time, 
and one who could give the Packers 
several years of consistent 
performance. 

Starr’s move paid off. Beverly went 
on to average 39.9 yards in 1977 and 
40.4 yards in 1979 and kept the 
Packers near the top of the league in 
punt coverage year after year. 

But when the artistry of Marcol and 
the consistency of Beverly began to 


didn’t want any more 


fade in recent years, those long-lost 
surprises started to creep back into 
the Packer repertoire. 

Some of the surprises found Starr 
as their victim. Some were produced 
by Starr himself. 

Surprise No. 1—It occurred 
during the 1979 training camp when 
Beverly, coming off a poor 1978 
season, was being challenged by 
rookie punter Rick Partridge. 
Partridge appeared to have the edge 
but when the final cuts were made, 
Beverly was still on the Packer roster 
and Partridge was on his way to New 
Orleans and eventually San Diego. 

Surprise No. 2—Marcol fell victim 
to a leg injury midway through the 
1979 season and, after missing a field

goal and extra points against the New 
York Jets at Lambeau Field, 
underwent surgery and missed the 
rest of the season. Starr picked up 
Tom Birney who had failed to make 
the Packer roster earlier that season. 

Surprise No. 3—Birney became 
the Packers’ Cinderella story of 1979, 
Expected to just be a mere fill-in for 
the injured Marcol, Birney made 
seven of nine field goal attempts in the 
1979 season, including four straight 
against the Lions in the final game of 
the season. 

Surprise No. 4—Marcol 
recovered from his injury and beat 
Birney out for the placekicking job at 
the start of the 1980 season. 

Surprise No. 5—It was the first 
game of the 1980 regular season and 
the Packers were trying to recover 
from a dismal exhibition season that 
ended with a 38-0 loss to the Denver 
Broncos. Marcol lined up to attempt 
what would be a game-winning field 


12 


Jan Stenerud awaits chance to kick as a new Packer vs. Tampa Bay in 


goal in the sudden death overtime 
against the Chicago Bears. Alan Page 
broke through to block the kick with 
his chest but the ball bounced back 
into Marcol’s arms. Marcol ran 
around left end for the touchdown to 
win the game, 12-6. 


Surprise No. 6—Just over four 
weeks later, Marcol was cut by Starr. 
Marcol had made two of his three field 
goal attempts and all seven extra point 
attempts. But his poor kickoffs were 
giving Packer opponents excellent 
field positions and Starr chose to 
make a change. 


Surprise No, 7—Rather than pick 
up another kicker from the free agent 
pool, Starr went back to Birney who 
was teaching school in Michigan. 


Surprise No. 8—Birney missed 
two relatively easy field goal attempts, 
one at the end of the fourth quarter 
and one at the end of the overtime 
period, and the Packers had to settle 
for a 14-14 tie against Tampa Bay. 


Surprise No. 9—Seven weeks 
later, Starr cut Birney. The decision 
came after Birney missed two extra 
points and a crucial 30-yard field goal 
in a 25-13 victory over the Minnesota 
Vikings. 

Surprise No. 10—Starr was able 
to convince veteran placekicker Jan 
Stenerud to sign a Packer contract for 
the remainder of the 1980 season. 
Stenerud had been cut by the Kansas. 
City Chiefs earlier in the season. 

When the 1980 season and all those 
surprises finally ended, the Packers’ 
kicking game, while not secure, 
seemed to be clear. 

Stenerud walked away from the 
final game at Detroit saying he very 
likely would not play again. Marcol 
had been claimed by the Houston 
Oilers but was later released and was 
in the throes of some personal 
problems. Birney would not be back. 
The placekicking job was wide open. 

The punting job did not seem tobea 
chief concern. Beverly averaged 38.2 
yards in 1980, had 18 kicks inside the 
20-yard line and didn’t seem to be in 
danger of losing his job. 

Then the final two surprises came 
along. In February, Stenerud 
telephoned Starr and told him he 
planned to report to training camp and 
asked to be given a fair chance to 
make the team. Starr gladly assured 
him of that chance. 

In late April, the Packers surprised 
many people by picking Michigan 
State punter Ray Stachowicz in the 
third round of the draft. 

Beverly asked for, and was given, 
his release from the Packers. 

Suddenly, the roles had changed. 
The placekicking game, expected to 
start from scratch in 1981, was in the 
capable hands, or feet, of a man 
regarded by some as the best NFL 
kicker of the 1970s. The punting 
game, while being entrusted to a 
kicker who gained nationwide 
prominence as a collegian, was 
starting anew, 

Dick Rehbein, the former DePere 
High School standout who has 
graduated to special teams coach for 
the Packers, is not surprised by the 
most recent changes. 

“T'm pleased that Jan is returning,” 
said Rehbein, who felt Stenerud was 
forced to kick under very difficult 
circumstances in the Packers’ final 
four games of 1980, “He still has good 
range and he’s been there. He knows 
what it’s like.” 


Stenerud, now 37-years-old, took a 
couple months to consider his future 
before phoning Starr with his decision 
and the reasons for it. 

“I told Bart after that last game that | 
would, in all likelihood, not come 
back,” Stenerud said. “But I told him 
Yd call him way before the draft to give 
him my final decision. 

“I came back for a number of 
reasons. First, while | haven't been 
around this team that long, from what 
I've seen | feel it has the potential to 
have a very good season. There are 
some holes to fill but with so many 
people getting healthy again, the team 
looks good and | feel it can win a lot of 
games. 

“Second, my wife and | like Green 
Bay, The people have been very nice 
to us and we like the people on the 
team. Third, I still enjoy the 
competition. There are a lot of good 
kickers around today but I feel I can 
still do the job and this is my 15th 
camp.” 

Rehbein said the Packers didn’t go 
into the draft determined to find a 
replacement for Beverly. 

“But we saw in this guy 
(Stachowicz) a chance to get a player 
who showed such outstanding 
potential as a collegiar,” Rehbein said. 
“It's difficult to make comparisons, 
and I wouldn't try to compare 


Stachowicz with someone like Ray 
Guy, but he has the capabilities of 
being a great punter for us.” 
Rehbein said the Packers were 
particularly pleased with Stachowicz' 


10 tie in Milwaukee in 1973. 


ability to kick under both adverse and 
pressurized kicking conditions. 

“He has shown an ability to kick in 
this climate,” Rehbein said, “which is 
important because you have to know 
how to kick into a wind and in cold 
weather. And he’s had to punt with his 
heels near the end line in front of 
100,000 people so he knows what 
pressure is like.” 

Stachowicz showed sharp 
improvement during his college career 
that saw him earn all-Big Ten honors 
four straight years. 

As a freshman, he averaged just 
38.8 yards a punt. He increased his 
average to 43.1 as a sophomore and 
finished up with 44.3 and 46.2 
averages. 

Several things accounted for his 
improvement. 

“Between my freshman and 
sophomore years, they changed 
footballs in college and that helped,” 
said Stachowicz. “I also adjusted to 
the wind at East Lansing, I had kicked 
in a valley in Ohio where it was cold, 
but there was no wind. © 

“Also, 1 got into stretching exercises 


a lot and developed my legs 
physically.” 
This could be Stenerud’s last 


season although he hedges by saying 
“Thate to go out ona limb and say it’s 
absolutely my last.” 

This could be the start of a long 
career for Stachowicz who Rehbein 
says “could very well play here for 10 
or 11 years,” 

But surprises do happen.

The Kick Story 


Fifty-one different players have kicked 
at least one point after touchdown or one 
field goal in the 62-year history of the 
Packers. 

Of that number, only 13 were kicking 
specialists. The first specialist employed 
by the Pack was Ben Agajanian, then in 
his 40's, who was called into replace Paul 
Hornung, who was in and out of the Army, 
in 1961 

Only two kickers followed him in the 
1960s, Don Chandler and Mike Mercer, 
The "K" specialists were numerous in the 
1970s but the most productive was 
Chester Marcol, who leads the Pack with 
515 kicking points. 

Other specialists were Dale Livingston. 
Tom Birney, Joe Danelo, Lou Michaels, 
Tim Webster, Booth Lusteg. Jan 
Stenerud, Dave Conway and Errol Mann

Jim Ringo (left) and Willie Davis were enshrined in the Hall of Fame at Canton, 0., Saturday, Aug. 1. 


Davis, Ringo 
Enter Canton 


WILLE DAVIS, blessed with the 
three attributes—speed agility and 
size—that Vince Lombardi 
considered most important for a 
successful football lineman. 

JIM RINGO, the only all-pro Vince 
Lombardi found when he took over 
the Packers in 1959. 

Davis and Ringo, two stalwarts of 
the Packers’ fabulous 1960s, were 
enshrined in the Pro Football Hall of 
Fame in Canton, Ohio on Aug. 1. 

Their entrance in the HOF brings to 
15 the number of former Packer stars 
who now are members of Pro 
Football’s Greatest Class. 

Eight of the 15 are from the 
Lombardi era, including Lombardi, 
himself, who was elected in 1971—a 
year after he died. The others are Jim 


14 


Taylor, Bart Starr, Forrest Gregg, Ray 
Nitschke, Herb Adderley and Davis 
and Ringo. 

Preceding them in Canton were Cal 
Hubbard, Don Hutson and Curly 
Lambeau of the charter class of 1963; 
Mike Michalske and Clarke Hinkle in 
1964; Arnie Herber in 1966; and Tony 
Canadeo in 1974. Walt Kiesling and 
Emlen Tunnell, who closed out their 
careers as Packers, also were named 
to the HOF. 

Besides speed, agility and size, 
Davis, a dynamic 6-3, 245-pound 
player, also had the intangible 
assets—dedication, intelligence, 
leadership—that enabled him to climb 
a cut above almost everyone else. In 
his 10 seasons as a Packer, Willie 
became widely recognized as a 


superior defensive end, one of the 
very best ever to play in the NFL. 

The Grambling graduate was an all- 
NFL selection five times in a six-year 
span, starting in 1962. He played in six 
NFL championship games and the 
first two Super Bowls. He was a Pro 
Bowl choice five years in a row. He 
played 12 seasons, the first two with 
Cleveland in 1958-59, and didn’t miss 
even one of the 162 games in that 
period. 

“He is a wonderful example of 
dedication, concentration and 
application and what it can do for an 
individual,” Phil Bengtson, who 
replaced Lombardi as the Green Bay 
coach in 1968, said. “He is a fine 
example for our younger players and

Ringo, who was an all-pro long 
before the Packers began dominating 
the NFL, played with the Packers 
from 1953 through 1963 and then with 
the Philadelphia Eagles from 1964 
through 1967. All-league seven of his 
11 years in Green Bay, he was named 
the top NFL center of the 1960s. 
During his combined Packers-Eagles 
tenure, he played in 10 Pro Bowl 
contests and was one of the few 
players ever to start for both the East 
and the West squads in the post- 
season classic 

Ringo, the Pack’s seventh round 
draft choice out of Syracuse in 1953, 
was one of the last of a vanishing breed 
in the NFL, a lineman who didn’t have 
the dimensions of a “jolly green giant.” 
He reported to the Packers 1953 
training camp at 21] pounds and never 
weighed more than 235 pounds at any 
time during his career. Yet he 
responded to the constant challenge 
of facing a much bigger opponent by 
utilizing his quickness and native 
gridiron intelligence and by learning all 
he could from coaches and 
teammates alike. 

Of Hungarian descent, Ringo was 
born Nov. 21, 1932, in Orange, N.J. He

played his high school football

How Packers 
Were Built 


Despite the additon of 10 free agents last year, the Packers are still a home- 
grown and young team. 

Coach Bart Starr, who decided in his first year (1975) to build the Packers 
through the draft, added six draftees in 1980 anc now has a total of 31 Packers 
draft choices going into the 1981 season. Twenty-nine of them were chosen by 
Starr and his staff. 

The big crop of free agents (10) last season resulted from a high injury total, 
especially at linebacker, plus an impressive free agent list. 

Of the 31 Packer-picked draftees, 18 are offensive players. Three of the six 
draft choices in 1980 were offensive linemen. 

Here, at a glance, is how the Packers were built and, don't forget, these 
athletes will be challenged by the 1981 gang of draft picks and free agents:

LYNN DICKEY STUCK 
ON FOOTBALL 


“Tlook at first, his love for the game. 
The guy just keeps coming back. He 
just don’t give up. You can’t help but 
haue respect for the guy. 

“T really envy the guy. He’s got a lot 
of heart, That's facts.” 

Eddie Lee Ivery, May 21, 1981 


By Tom Wheatley 
Green Bay Press-Gazette 


It hurts to be in love with pro 
football. 

And Green Bay Packer quarter- 
back Lynn Dickey is the textbook 
example of a battered player. 

The hurt, of course, can be 
physical. Dickey, who will be 32 in 
October, has spent three of his 
previous 10 pro seasons mending on 
the sidelines. Although his high schoo! 
in Kansas named its football field after 
him, the local hospital might have 
been a better choice. 

Dickey made it through 1980 
without a career-threatening crackup 
on the field, like the hip injury he 
suffered in 1972, or the shoulder 
surgery in 1976, or his shattered leg in 
1977, 

But last season also showed how 
football can cause internal injuries. 

After Green Bay's third game, a 51 
21 loss to Los Angeles, Dickey 
suffered what seemed to be an 
emotional crackup. 

He had thrown three interceptions, 
one of them a 99-yard scoring return 
by Los Angeles rookie Johnnie 
Johnson. Afterward, Dickey 
questioned his ability to lead the 
Packers, 

Reacting to Dickey’s comment, 
Packer Coach Bart Starr questioned 
Dickey’s professionalism. 

But football, like lave, is fickle. 

Dickey regrouped after the Los 
Angeles game to have by far his most 
productive season. 

The Packers, then 1-2 and 
floundering offensively, came up with 
a new game plan for Dickey. 

Dickey came up witha new outlook. 
And the Packers came up with some 


big yardage totals in their last 13 
games. 

They continued at a win one, lose 
two pace with their new ball-control 
passing game. But the problems that 
led to the team’s 4-8-1 over that 
stretch were not Dickey-related, as he 
suggested after the Ram game. He had 
vindicated himself. Not that his 
teammates felt that was necessary. 

While the fans, press and even the 
Packer coaching staff wondered (f 
Dickey had finally run up the white flag 
after the Ram game, the Packer 
players knew better. 

“I can’t remember anybody trying 
to badmouth the guy and say, ‘Lynn 
Dickey's not the man,’ recalled 
running back Eddie Lee Ivery. “He 
couldn't get the team rolling 
offensively, and the guy takes it upon 
himself to take the entire blame. 
That's just the kind of guy he is. 

“You can't help but admire him. | 
feel every guy on the team has 
admiration for the guy, ‘cause he just 
keeps pushin’ it. It’s just a great lesson 
for young guys like myself.” 

“T think everyone respects him, [ 
do,” said linebacker John Anderson. 
“He definitely has a lot of guts to come 
back.” 

Did Dickey’s comments after the 
Ram game lower his stature in the 
team's eyes? “It didn’t in mine,” 
snapped Anderson.

The Passing in 1980 


“It’s so obvious that the man’s got a 
lot of determination, got a lot of will,” 
said safety Johnnie Gray. “He’savery 
self-disciplined person, a strong 
leader. He knows what he wants to get 
done on the field. And I think he's 
proven it last year by the way he 
played. To me, he’s a superstar.” 

Dickey downplays his role as an 
inspirational leader. “I haven't really 
thought about it,” he said. As for 
coming back from injuries, “I had 
more practice,” he said. 

His sense of humor has kept him 
going through his tortured career, 
After separating his shoulder against 
the Bears in November 1976, an injury 
which required surgery and ended his 
season, he said: “It was another day at 
the office.” 

But he does take his injuries 
seriously, They have stoked, rather 
than sapped, his love for the game. 

“Fortunately one way, unfortunate- 
ly in another, 1 was pushed to the 
limit,” said Dickey of his severe 
injuries. “I either had to respond, or 
just cash it in, give it up. 

“The problem with a lot of football 
teams is, the players don’t know what 
they have to put out tomake a winning 
season a reality. They slap each other 
around and yell rah-rah. And they're 
talking about putting in the sweat, 
putting in the time. But a lot of people,

19 


David Whitehurst, now a 5-year veteran Packer quarterback, monitors game strategy being discussed by Lynn Dickey 
and Backtield Coach Zeke Bratkowski. 


Continued from Page 19 

they've never been pushed beyond 
that point. So when they get there, 
they stop. They fall short. 

“We're that kind of football team. 
There are a couple of guys who come 
under that heading. I know. I can 
remember being there myself.” 

How did he crash through that 
barrier? 

“Well, I guess I’ve been nearer to 
retirement than I care to think about,” 
said Dickey. “Anda little click goes off 
in your mind: ‘Hey, it’s over.’ You geta 
quick flash of those. 

“And | just said. ‘If I'm gonna go 
down, I’m gonna give it all I've got.’” 

That’s what made the Ram game so 
frustrating. Here he was, reasonably 
healthy at last, leading the Packers 
into reverse and at high speed. 

Dickey can laugh now about his 
postgame comments. 

As Dickey said after his comments 
first hit the papers, he wasn’t 
misquoted. But he was misunder- 
stood. 

“Td been making a mistake in my 
play up to that point, and wasn’t 
playing well,” said Dickey. “I had been 
distrusting what everybody else was 
gonna do and really not taking care of 
my own job. I was worrying about 
other people making mistakes, and I 
was the one making the majority of 
them.” 


20 


But he couldn't fill that foreman 
role. The result was his postgame 
quote in Anaheim that “I don’t know if 
I can help this team.” 

“T felt like I can’t take this team toa 
winning position. 1 can’t do it by 
myself. If somebody can do that 
maybe,” explained Dickey. “But I’m 
not that guy for this team. And 
immediately, everybody came out and 
said, ‘Boy, has he lost confidence in 
himself. Will he be able to play at all?’ 
had buddies of mine I know real well 
and goof around with in Kansas City 
(where he now lives) call me. And it 
came across to them the same way, 
that I just rock-bottom lost confidence 
in myself and that I was ready to give 
the game up. 

“But I never lost confidence in 
myself.” 

Dickey didn’t retreat after the Ram 
game. He just attacked in a different 
direction. “After.the game, I kind of 
said. ‘The hell with everybody else. I’m 
just gonna take care of my job, and 
just take it for granted that everyone 
else is gonna take care of his job.’ [just 
put blinders on. 

“Zeke (quarterback coach Zeke 
Bratkowski) has mentioned to all the 
quarterbacks at times, ‘Be selfish.’ 
And that’s the way I approached the 
game. Surprisingly, I played better, 
and the team responded better.” 

Both Dickey and the team 


responded well to the quick rhythm 
passing game put in after the Ram 
debacle. It stressed short passes. The 
theory, said Dickey, “is just keep 
moving the chains, and pretty soon 
the goal line’s gonna hit you in the 
face.” 

The strategy fit in with Dickey’s new 
selfish philosophy. Before, he felt 
obligated to force the ball downfield to 
a primary receiver, even if he were 
well-covered. 

“But if you’ve got aback standing at 
the line of scrimmage there’s a 
completion. Give him the football and 
let him shake and bake and see what 
he can do. And all of asudden, he gets 
away and he’s got an 8-yard gain for 
you. 

“If you figure how hard you gotta 
work for a 5-yard run consistently, 
you've gotta take that. You can’t live 
and die with it. You've got to throw 
deep and throw the little longer 
rhythm passes just to keep the 
defense honest. But you can plan a 
large portion of your passing game ina 
quick rhythm system.” 

Dickey said he always liked the 
quick rhythm game, but refused to 
take credit for selling it to Starr, 
Bratkowski and receiver coach Lew 
Carpenter. “If I had solely come up 
with the idea, I’d say so,” he laughed. 
“But it didn’t happen like that.” 

Packer fans can expect that offense 


again this year. 

“You can’t really can something 
that worked for you,” said Dickey, 
who also likes the way it reduces wear 
and tear on quarterbacks. “It cuts 
down on the opportunity to getting 
sacked,” he said. “The linemen can 
hit, and hit, and usually the ball’s gone, 
rather than having to sustain a 
defensive lineman for four seconds.” 

He may have avoided major injury, 
but Dickey still managed his share of 
physical hurts. He racked up a leg 
twice, first in the exhibition opener 
and then again late in the season. 

In between, he developed tendinitis 
in his throwing arm. For a six-week 
stretch, the pain kept him from 
throwing for three days after a game. 
Right after the game, in fact, his 
shoulder was so sore he could barely 
put his sports coat on. 

So why does he keep torturing 
himself? 

Money probably is one reason. His 
contract is believed to be for nearly 
$200,000 this year. He has the 
freedom now to spend his off-seasons 
now doing what he likes best, working 
out, playing golf and tending his 
investments. 

In four or five years, if his 
investments pan out, he probably 
would be financially free for life, 
Dickey said. 

But the money isn’t worth the risk of 
not walking again, a possibility after 
his hip injury in ’72. 

“The money's fine, but it comes and 
goes,” said Dickey. 

“Look, I'm not fast. I never claimed 
to be. But I feel I've got enough 


Mark Miller, left, obtained in a trade with the Browns, and Mike Mancuso, 
former Cowboy, are in running for a Packer quarterback relief job behind Lynn 


Dickey. 

quickness to play my position and play 
it well, and to get out of trouble and 
make something happen. If it got to 
the sitting-duck stage, yeah, I'd think 
about hanging it up. I've seen guys at 
that stage in their careers when I 
think, ‘Wow, it’s fun. But it ain’t that 
much fun.’ 

“T enjoy the game, the physical part 
and the mental preparation. The 
things that have happened to me are 
bad breaks. They happen. Some 
people never get a scratch. I’ve just 
been in the wrong place at the right 
time. 

“But I want to be able to say in 10 
years, when I’m approaching 42, 
‘When I was able to play, physically, [ 
playedas longas! could. And now that 
I’m done, I don’t miss it a bit.’ 

“T don’t want to say, ‘Boy, I could 
have played another three or four 
years, if only | had given it the effort.’” 

Dickey’s wife, Sherry, isn’t crazy 
about seeing him hauled off the field 


on a stretcher at least annually, he 
admitted. “Obviously, it upsets her,” 
he said. “But I think I'd have everyone 
nuts around my house (he has three 
daughters) if I quit the game and still 
had a lot of it in me. Once I rehab and 
get back on my feet again, she’s all for 
anything that I want to do.” 

And there isn’t anything else he’d 
rather do. “I don’t want to get ajob in 
the offseason just to say, ‘Hey, I got a 
job.’” said Dickey, a phys ed major at 
Kansas State. 

His life is football. The game is in his 
blood, “which is all over the field,” he 
said, grinning. 

His goal is a Super Bowl ring. “It 
doesn’t hurt to dream,” he said. “If it 
doesn’t happen, I’m not gonna roll 
over and die. You gotta shoot for 
something. 

“Everybody's got the loves of their 
lives, what they enjoy doing.” 

And Lynn Dickey, for better or for 
worse, is stuck on football.

CAMPBELL 
- A PURE 
ves PASSER 


Rich Campbell, a Packer rarity in a 
historical sense, is happy about being 
a Packer. 

The Packers’ rookie is only the 
eighth quarterback chosen as a first 
choice by Green Bay in 45 years of the 
NFL college player draft. 

The previous quarterbacks chosen 
in the first round never made a big 
mark in Green Bay as a QB. Only one 
was a spectacular, but not at 
quarterback. That would be Paul 
Hornung, who played his first year at 
QB, and then was switched to running 
back. 

The others were Ernie Case, Stan 
Heath, Babe Parilli, Randy Duncan, 
Don Horn and Jerry Tagge. Case and 
Duncan never played in Green Bay, 
Heath made it for one year, Horn and 
Tagge four years and Parilli five. 

Being a No. 1 draft choice in this 
particular period of pro football 
carries with it a might more pressure 
than it did 15 or 20 years ago. 

When Campbell reported for the 
Pack’s mini-camp in May, Backfield 
Coach Zeke Bratkowski touched on 
one of the young QB's problems: 
“Everything is difficult for him right 
, = now because there’s so much media 
Rich Campbell tests arm at Packers’ mini-camp. pressure. Everybody wants to talk to 


22 


Gary Lewis 


him. That’s going to be an adjustment 
he’s going to have to make the first 
two or three days. And then he’ll be 
able to relax and concentrate.” 

Coach Bart Starr and Bratkowski 
both said they intend to bring 
Campbell along slowly and watch his 
development carefully. He'll be in 
competition with David Whitehurst, 
Mark Miller, Mike Mancuso and 
Nickie Hall for one of the two backup 
spots behind number one quarter- 
back Lynn Dickey. 

At 6-4 and 224 pounds, Campbell is 
the ideal size for a quarterback in the 
pros. And he has an advantage over 
other rookie QB prospects. 

His school used a_pro-style 
offense—plus most of the teams he 
played against used pro-type 
coverages. He responded with a 70.7 
completion percentage in 1980 and 
64.5 for three seasons. Both 


Bill Whitaker 


Richard Turner 


percentages are NCAA records. 

Campbell is considered a “pure” 
passer in that he has exceptional 
accuracy, touch and timing. He has a 
great deal of patience and rarely 
“forces” the ball to a receiver. 

Roger Theder, Campbell’s coach at 
California, thinks he may be a hit 
sooner than many people think. “I 
think Rich will be a great quarterback 
coming in,” Theder said, adding: 
“There is not that much about pro 
coverages that Rich will not already 
have seen, and his preference was 
Green Bay.” 

Campbell was really excited about 
being picked by the Packers, as 
evidenced by his off the cuff remarks 
right after being drafted. Later, Rich 
said “It’s true. My comments about 
coming to Green Bay were spoken 
from the heart.” 

A hunter and fisherman, Campbell 


Byron Braggs 


said “in the Bay (San Francisco) area 
you have to drive two hours to do 
something like that. Now in Green 
Bay I can do that in half an hour. I like 
that type of atmosphere, this type of 
town.” 

This was the second year in a row 
the Pack didn’t draft a running back, 
but they did select a second 
quarterback, the aforementioned 
Hall, who was chosen on the 10th 
round. 

Hall never played much until he was 
a senior at Tulane and then threw 21 
touchdown passes. He has 
considerable raw tallent and his arm 
has been called “a real whip” by Dick 
Corrick, director of player personnel. 

Here are the Packers’ 1981 draft 
choices and the figures in parentheses 
designate the order of selection of the

By Lee Remmel, Green Bay Packer Director of Public Relations 


“] have a vivid memory of the first 
number Equipment Manager Dad 
Braisher gave me when | arrived in 
Green Bay. It didn’t make me feel very 
confident because it was 42. | thought 
to myself, ‘There’s no way they think 
I’m going to be around very long, with 
a number like that.’” 

The memory drew a throaty and 
heartfelt chuckle from Bart Starr, 
fondly reflecting upon that early day 


from the panoramic perspective of 25 
years’ association with Green Bay and 
the Packers. 

The number in question, long since 
changed and all but forgotten, hardly 
had been a barometer of things to 
come. Not only did the youthful 
southerner survive, he went to play for 
16 seasons—longer, it turned out, 
than any other Packer in the team’s 
62-year existence. The rest of the 


story is familiar. In the ensuing 
process, he matured into the most 
productive quarterback in the history 
of professional football, escorting 
Green Bay to five National Football 
League titles in a memorable seven- 
year span. 

And now, after a two-year 
sabbatical as a fan, combined with 
weekend employment as a CBS-TV 

Continued on Page 26 


25 


Continued from Page 25 

football analyst, he finds himself in his 
seventh year as head coach of the 
game’s most intriguing franchise. 

As a_ perennial and practicing 
exponent of the positive approach— 
even the effervescent Ronald Reagan 
pales by comparison—Bart Starr is 
not one tolive in the past. A doer anda 
builder by nature, he is too engrossed 
in maximizing the present and 
planning for the future to spend much 
time on “history.” 

While zeroing in on the 1981 
season, however, the storied ex-field 
general did permit himself to look 
back over those 25 years of green and 
gold, and what they have wrought—in 
the Packers, in the game, and in his 
life. 

When the clean-cut and courtly son 
of Alabama first arrived in Green Bay, 
Dwight David Eisenhower was 
nearing the end of his first term as 
President and the Packers were on the 
verge of their final season in compact 
and venerable City Stadium, 
situationed on the banks of East River, 
where it had been the mecca for 
Wisconsin's pro football buffs since 
1925 

Bart may never forget that maiden 
visit. “I was here around the first of 
July in 1956 for the first time,” he says. 
“T was very nervous, but very excited 
and was, obviously, quite surprised 
because we got one of those blasts of 
coolair that you can get here that time 
of year 

“T had never been this far north at 
that time of year, and quite frankly, 
wasn't prepared for it. And, even 
though we were only walking between 
the Northland Hotel and the YMCA, 
where we were eating our meals, | 
almost froze to death a couple of 
mornings at breakfast. That memory 
lingers with me yet today.” 

His frigid introduction to the home 
of the Packers was not the only 
surprise the young quarterback was 
to get. “I was extremely shocked at 
the size—or the lack of size—of the 
dressing room,” he says. “We were, in 
those days, playing at City Stadium, 
which was behind East High School. 
When we walked into the dressing 
room for the first time, I couldn't 
believe how small it was.” 

Although Bart found his first 
training camp “very, very exciting,” he 
notes, “I was already lonely at that 
time because [ had been gone from 
home for a couple weeks. | was also 
concerned. We had a couple of very 


26 


strong-armed quarterbacks who I 
could see were quality players. In 
those days, the word was out that they 
only kept two quarterbacks, and I 
knew they had a very experienced, 
excellent leader in Tobin Rote. So I 
didn’t know what to expect, except to 
give it my best shot. As concerned and 
as excited as I was, | was also 
extremely confident because I had 
worked extremely hard coming into 
that first training camp. 

“l had spent the entire summer 
getting ready and I really believe that 
enabled me to get off to a flying start 
because the other quarterbacks here 
at the time were stronger physically, 


and perhaps even better passers. 
However they hadn’t worked as hard 
as I had and, as a result, I stayed and 
they didn’t. 

One moment which is still green in 
Starr’s memory occurred in early 
August that ’56 season. The occasion 
of his first full-dress outing ina Green 
Bay uniform. “It was in an intra-squad 
game in Janesville,” he said. “We 
played two intra-squad games in that 
year—one in Janesville and one in 
Neenah-Menasha. | was fortunate 
enough to play well in both of them 
and, again, | think those performances 
enabled me to stay and make the 
team. Each time you performed well,

of course, it was just a reinforcing of 
your position. 

“T threw a pass for a potential score 
to Billy Howton, a great wide receiver, 
and he dropped it in the end zone. He 
came back to the huddle and said, 
‘Rook’—he didn’t even call me by 
name—‘that was a hell of a fine throw, 
and I'll never drop another one for 
you.’ And he didn’t.” 

As might have been expected, Bart 
is quick to admit the late Vince 
Lombardi had a profound influence 
upon him. But, somewhat 
surprisingly, he gives the bulk of the 
credit for his development as a 
quarterback to another. 

“T learned a lot from a number of 
people,” he concedes, “but I think it 
has to go all the way back to just prior 
to my senior year in high school, when 
I spent an extensive period of time at 
the University of Kentucky under 
Babe Parilli. He taught me as much 
about the fundamentals of the position 
as anyone ever has. He was the only 
real quarterback who had ever 
worked with me. Other coaches had 
spent time with me, but they had 
never played the position, so he was 
directly responsible for my 
fundamental growth in that particular 
position. 

“When I came here, naturally I had 
the opportunity of observing the 
people who had played the game for a 
number of years. | think the greatest 
attribute I developed in observing 
Tobin Rote was courage. I’ve never 
seen a more courageous individual. 
He didn’t enjoy the protection at that 
time that many quarterbacks did—he 
took a real beating from time to time— 
but I never heard him whimper.To the 
contrary, he was an outgoing, 
demonstrative, excellent leader, who 
almost welcomed the challenge of the 
shots that he would take. He was as 
courageous a player as I’ve ever been 
around.” 

The indestructible Rote also figured 
in one of Starr’s most indelible off-the- 
field memories. “Mr. Quarterback” 
had gone into service early in 1957, 
following his rookie season, but 
shortly had been released because ofa 
chronic back condition before the ’57 
season started. 

“TI got back here and was playing golf 
with Tobin two days before training 
camp opened,” Bart remembers. “We 
were playing at the Town and Country 
Golf Course and someone from the 
Packer office drove up out there to tell

Here is Bart Starr in a typical work pose — bending under the center and bark-

Bart Starr works with young quarterback in practice. Is Coach Vince Lombardi listening? 


Continued from Page 27 

Tobin he had been traded to the 
Lions. I've never seen a guy so upset, 
and I couldn’t blame him, because 
that’s a heck of away to find out. He 
was so mad that he actually drove his 
tee shot over the green on the short 
par 4 first hole.” 

It might be assumed Rote’s depar- 
ture provided Starr with smooth sailing 
professionally, but such was not the 
case. “Babe Parilli had been brought 
back here that same '57 season in a 
trade with Cleveland,” Bart points 
out, “and I found myself in a very 
awkward situation, because he had 
been my idol in high school. | had 
worked with him—he trained me— 
and here I was competing with him. It 
was an uneasy situation for two years. 
We became involved in a musical 
chairs arrangement whereby one 
would be benched if he were not 
performing well. | think each of us 
suffered some loss of confidence as a 
result and the ultimate loser was the 
team.” 

Two years later, Lombardi arrived 
upon the Green Bay scene and the 
course of Packer history was about to 
change dramatically. So, it turned out, 
was the football life of one Bart Starr. 


28 


I think there are two things! learned 
from Coach Lombardi that stand out 
quickly,” Bart says. “One was 
organization. It was so much better 
organized than in 1958. I was three 
feet off the ground following our initial 
meeting with Coach Lombardi. 

“The second most important lesson 
I learned from him—one that stood up 
all the years that he was here—was a 
true commitment to excellence— 
giving the very best you have. I’ve 
never been able to understand how 
someone can give less than his best 
effort. 

“The reason I welcomed this 
approach and his feelings toward it is 
that my dad has always preached the 
same thing. I don’t know if some of 
Coach Lombardi’s emphasis was due 
to his exposure to Earl Blaik at West 
Point or not, but I know my dad’s 
military experience seemed to parallel 
that of some leaders Lombardi 
admired most. My dad preached one 
theme throughout our lives as we 
were growing up as youngsters—total 
commitment. And here came Coach 
Lombardi—it was like hearing my 
father all over again. It was really great 
to hear. 

Bart matter-of-factly describes his 


relationship with Lombardi as 
“businesslike.” 

“Tt was a good coach-player 
relationship,” he said. “I never 
attempted to get very close to him, 
and I was never encouraged to do so. 
A couple of players were able to get 
very close over the years—Paul 
(Hornung) and Max (McGee). Their 
personalities were such and the 
positions they played such that I don’t 
think he even objected to it. He almost 
welcomed it. He was always able to 
remain aloof when necessary, but I 
think he welcomed the opportunity to 
be a little closer to some players than 
others. 

“Ours was strictly a relationship 
along business lines. It was always a 
very comfortable one, never stilted or 
uneasy. It was warm, but very 
businesslike during the first two or 
three years. But, with each passing 
year, our meetings were more like 
father-son gatherings.” 

There seems little question 
Lombardi was a truly great coach, it 
was interposed. But why, specifically, 
was he? 

“He was extremely demanding, 
because he demanded a great deal of 
himself. He was an outstanding 


teacher. | think his teaching 
experience in high school enabled him 
to present subject matter clearly and 
concisely in the classroom. I've always 
felt, too, that though a coach may 
excel in one area or the other, he 
should teach well in the classroom and 
on the practice field. |'ve been around 
coaches who were outstanding in the 
classroom and terrible on the field, or 
superb on the field and poor in the 
classroom. Your truly professional 
coaches have to be good in both 
areas, in order to qualify as great 
teachers. Our present staff is 
composed of coaches who take pride 
in their ability to teach. They reflect 
our emphasis on securing good people 
who can teach. 


“Coach Lombardi was extremely 
knowledgeable—he knew the game 
very well. We were—I think—ahead of 
the league at the time in audibilizing 
and taking advantage of defenses. 


“Defenses in those days were too 
quick to tip what they were going to 
do. They showed ‘dog’ and blitz far too 
early. They positioned their 
alignments in such a fashion that you 
could audibilize and take advantage of 
them. That rarely happens today. So 
we were, | think, ahead of the league 
and we stayed ahead of them for a 
while. They slowly caught up to that 
sort of thing, but we really hurt teams 
with audibles for years. 


“Additionally, 1 thought his timing 
was outstanding. | don’t know that he 
was ever inappropriate with his 
comments. There were times we as 
players thought he was, but, as we 
analyzed the film, we discovered he 
was correct in his assessment.” 


The highly articulate Alabamian has 
no trouble recalling that state of his 
emotions when, under Lombardi, the 
No. 1 assignment at quarterback 
became a tantalizing possibility for 
him. 

“Tt started from the very first 
meeting with Coach Lombardi in the 
downtown offices,” Bart confided. “I 
was walking on air; | was extremely 
excited. I could see a change coming 
even then. It continued through 
training camp. I was working primarily 
behind Lamar McHan who had been 
dealt here by the Cardinals. Joe 
Francis and Babe Parilli, of course, 
were the other quarterbacks. As 
training camp wound down, Parilli 
went to play in Canada and the three

of us remained. McHan became the 
starting quarterback and led us to a 
victory in the opener against the 
Bears. 

“Though we started strong, we hit 
the skids and lost five games ina row 
in the middle of the season. When we 
went to New York City to meet the 
Giants, Lamar was suffering from a 
sore shoulder and was not able to 
start. 1 thought I'd get a chance to play 
that day; I was excited about the 
opportunity because I knew what it 
meant to Coach Lombardi to be 
playing in New York City, his home 
town, against the Giants, his old team. 

“Lo and behold, when Mac couldn't 
perform that day, Lombardi called on 
doe. I sat on the bench, crushed 
because I ‘knew’ I'd play that day. Asa 
result, | was extremely disappointed 
and very dejected after the ball game. 
We stayed over night in New York and 
T went out with Ron Kramer that 
night,” Bart said, adding with a deep 
chuckle, “and I don’t remember 
coming in. 

“A week later, when Lamar couldn’t 
play again, Lombardi gave me a 
chance to play and the fun began.” 

In the decade that followed, 
Number 15 was to preside at the 
offensive controls in a host of big 
games, including Super Bowls I and II, 
in both of which he was chosen the 
Most Valuable Player. But the latter 
are not the only ones that stand out in 
his memory. 

“IT think that your championship 
games probably are the ones you 
always tend to reflect on first,” Bart 
acknowledges, “but there were a 
couple of others that I vividly 
remember—for different reasons. 
There was a final game against San 
Francisco in 1959, when Lew 
Carpenter was playing fullback 
because Jimmy Taylor had burned 
himself in an accident at home. To 
show you the versatility of Carpenter, 
he rushed for over one hundred yards 
that day, and we literally cut up the 
49ers because of our outstanding 
game plan. 

“When you could equate to the 
design of a game plan, truly 
understand what that game plan 
afforded you and how you were able to 
take advantage of the other team, 
then that game was memorable. 
Needless to say, we won going away. 

“Another game which I'll never 
forget was the final game in Los 
Angeles in 1960. By defeating the 


30 


rs! 


Bart Starr was a spectator in 1973 
and 1974. 


Rams. that day, we would be in the 
championship game in only our 
second year under Lombardi. Gary 
Knafelc and I were roommates and so 
excited about that game neither of us 
slept much the night before. We were 
up so early the next morning no one 
was on the streets of Santa Monica. 
We must have walked up and down 
the beach for two hours before the 
pre-game meal, left it early, went to get 
taped early. We just accelerated 
everything. 

“By doing so, we missed an 
announcement at the pre-game meal 
that the bus departure had been 
pushed up 15 minutes. Well, we left 
our room to go what we thought was 
15 minutes early to catch the buses, 
and we're late. Through the glass 
doors of the lobby, we could see 
Coach Lombardi standing by the first 
bus, and we could tell he was upset 
even from a distance. You could see 
the steam coming out of his ears. He 
spotted us through the doors and 
literally ran to grab us. He popped one 
door open and blurted some 


expletives. He said that we were being 
fined and to get our ass on the bus. | 
kept telling myself on the trip to the 
Coliseum, ‘Don't let this upset you; 
don’t let it get to you; you’ve got a 
game plan to execute.” 

“After we arrived, Gary and I were 
dressing together in the same cubicle. 
There was hardly a word exchanged, 
for obvious reason. We went out, won 
the ball game, played extremely well. 
After the game, Coach Lombardi 
came by, stuck his head in the cubicle 
and said, ‘Forget the fine.’ He didn’t 
say, ‘Nice game, well played,’ 
anything. Just ‘forget the fine.’ As he 
walked away, Gary said, ‘Thanks a lot, 
you old sonofabitch.’” Again, Bart 
“broke up” at the memory, adding, 
“That has to be one of the most 
meaningful experiences I've ever had. 
Gary and | still laugh about it.” 

There also was a season, Starr will 
tell you, he will never forget. 

“| think the °67 season was 
meaningful for us, because we were 
crippled and hurt,” he said. “It 
probably was the greatest example of 
teamwork on display throughout a 
year that I've ever been a part of— 
because we really should not have 
won that year. 

“It’s quite possible another team 
could because of our 
injuries. We were hurt in a number of 
areas, but found a way to win, and 
that's what you have to do. In 
retrospect, it was very satisfying 
because of the obstacles we 
overcame, beating Dallas in the “Ice 
Bowl” and capping the season with a 
big Super Bowl win was the highlight 
of a very hard fought year. We were 
literally drained at the end of that 
season. It was a very emotional year 
for us.” 

Ironically, the then veteran 
quarterback encountered the greatest 
disappointment of his playing career 
the same season. 

“| think the injury to my shoulder in 
1967, from a personal standpoint, 
handicapped me from playing well at a* 
time when I felt | was just peaking 
mentally,” he says. “I'm quite 
confident that, from a_ physical 
standpoint, excluding my shoulder, 
that I could have played for many 
years after that. | felt good, | had 
always kept myself in good shape. I 
had reached a point where mentally I 
just felt like I could—with the proper 
supporting cast—go out and eat up 
another team. That’s a great feeling 
for a quarterback to have. 


have won 


“But, from °67 on, [ couldn’t do that 
because I couldn’t throw the ball like I 
wanted to. [ remember the year I tried 
to come back and play against New 
Orleans in Milwaukee (1971, his 16th 
and last season), | had a couple of 
passes picked off by people who 
shouldn’t have been anywhere near 
the ball. I knew then that I couldn't 
perform after that.” 

Though he ordinarily eschews 
comparisons, particularly of teams 
and/or individuals, Starr readily 
admits football has changed 
dramatically since he first moved in 
under center for the Packers a quarter 
century ago. 

“The game is played at a faster 
pace,” he says without equivocation. 
“The coaching/teaching is better. 
Everything is more sophisticated due 
to increased emphasis on situation 
planning from the use of substitutions 
to the deployment of those players. I 
don’t know that the top athletes are 
any better; but they are bigger, 
stronger and faster. I think they 
receive better total conditioning 
advice from an earlier age today, and 
by the time they reach this level, many 
are awesome. On the other hand, we 
have some on rosters who could not 
have played 20 years ago. 

“Reemphasizing an earlier point, 
preparation time has expanded, for 
both coaches and players, because 
the game is so situation-oriented. 
Everything is geared to situations 
today. Some teams will hit you with a 
‘nickel’ defense if you have more than 
seven or eight yards to go on second 
down. It’s incredible to see defenses 
change and the number of looks that 


The program for Bart Starr Day. 


you get; I'm obviously speaking 
offensively here. From the defensive 
side, you get the same thing; you see 
offenses come in with different people, 
if you get behind in the down and 
distance game.” 

Is it possible, Starr was asked, that 
pro football’s popularity has peaked? 

“T believe we have enjoyed some of 
the greatest popularity the game will 
know,” he replied. “I don’t know that 
we've necessarily peaked, because 
through sophisticated electronic 
communications, we display a sport to 
millions of people all over the world. 

“T think that management's greatest 
challenges in the coming years are to 


heighten imagination, explore new 
marketing ideas, and structure 
soundly with people who are team 
oriented, unselfish, committed and 
knowledgeable.” 

Narrowing the focus, to his own 
team and the immediate future, Starr 
sights better days ahead. “I believe 
we'll have a much better football team 
this season simply because | believe 
we're going to be healthy—and more 
consistent. If we are, we'll have 
improved performances. We'll 
continue our building—and [I’m 
speaking not only of the team but our 
own physical plant and our growth as 
an organization. 

“We have a lot of growth to engage 
in here in the coming years. If we’re 
going to be competitive within the 
National Football League, there are 
numerous changes that must be 
made. 

“T feel this year’s team will be the 
most talented we've had since I've 
been head coach here because we've 
been able to draft and acquire more 
capable players. We’re still deficient in 
a couple of critical areas because we 
haven't been able to fillin to the degree 
that we would like. But in time we'll be 
just fine.” 

He obviously enjoys coaching, it 
was suggested. Does he plan to 
continue indefinitely? 

“Yes, I do. And I'd like to continue 
coaching here for some time. By 
winning this year, I believe we'll have 
that opportunity. It would then be a 
goal of all of us to continue the building 
of this football team toward a 
championship; that’s what we’re all 
after.”
