The Packers Won It All In Green Bay 
Twenty Years Ago — a Personal Look 


Don Langenkamp 
32 


By Don Langenkamp 
Green Bay Press-Gazette 


Twenty years... 

Can it really be 20 years since that 
day when the first National Football 
League championship game was 
played in Green Bay? 

And can it be 20 years since I last 
wore the good old blue and white for 
Merrill High? 

Twenty years since Vince 
Lombardi’s Packers laid claim to 
supremacy of the football world for 
the first time? 

Twenty years since I helped the 


Bluejays stumble through a 1-7 
season? 

Can it really be two decades since 
the New York Giants—those big-city 
bad guys from the East Coast— 
swaggered into town, only to exit a 
thoroughly thrashed and chagrined 
team? 

Twenty years since this mediocre 
lineman ingloriously ended his 
gridiron career by suffering a broken 
toe during the last practice of his 
senior year? 

It can be, and it is. Two decades 
have somehow rushed past since this 
high school senior saw and heard the 


2n Bay. Blocking for six-yard run are Fuzzy Thurston (63), Norm Masters (78), Jim Taylor (31) and Forrest Gregg (helmet showing). 


first chapter of the Packers’ Glory 
Years written. Twenty years have 
amazingly evaporated since those 
heady Sunday afternoons of 1961 
when Packer success was not yet 
demanded, but merely savored as a 
rare delicacy by Packer fans. 

Vince Lombardi was not yet a 
legend, but merely a worthy adversary 
for the likes of George Halas or Paul 
Brown. Paul Hornung’s image was just 
beginning to get its golden tint, Bart 
Starr’s star was still on the rise, and 
even Fuzzy Thurston looked youthful. 

It’s been 20 years all right. Since 

Continued on Page 34 


33 


Continued from Page 33 


then we have reveled through the 
Sixties and become a jaundiced lot in 
the Seventies. We saw that fragile 
thing called success come to Green 
Bay and stay longer than it had 
anywhere before in National Football 
League annals. And before it wafted 
away, we counted ourselves among 
football's chosen fans. 

The beginning of that era was 1961. 
More specifically, it was Dec. 31, 1961, 
when the Packers did a 37-0 number 
on the aging but still-potent New York 
Giants. 

But let’s back up just a bit here. 
Let’s go back to the beginning of our 
seasons—mine and the Packers’— 
that year. It didn’t take long to set the 
tone for the fall. The Packers were 
winning their games Sunday after 
Sunday; we were losing ours Friday 
night after Friday night. 

I began to use the Packer 
performances as salve for the wounds 
suffered two days before. | somehow 
felt a weekly exposure to their brand 
of excellence would lift our 
beleaguered high school team to 
previously unscaled heights—and 
maybe even a victory or two. But, 
apparently, our opponents in Antigo 
and Schofield and Wisconsin Rapids 
were watching and_listening—and 
better emulating—the Packers as 
well. 

We would shrug off our Friday night 
misadventures and by Sunday 
morning, wait anxiously for the 
kickoff. We began to sense that the 
1960 appearance in the championship 
game against the Philadelphia Eagles 
was no fluke, that this team was 
indeed the genuine article. 

Dynasty was not yet part of our 
vernacular then. The Packers hadn’t 
established theirs yet, and when you 
play on a 1-7 team, you don’t think 
about dynasties, you simply weigh 
your chances for survival each week. 
But as Vince always did, we played 
’em one at a time, respectably if not 
successfully. 

No, the success story was 120 miles 
away in Green Bay. And, finally, after 
Antigo put the final nail in our coffin of 
a campaign, | turned my full attention 
to what was happening with the 
Packers. 

This winning habit the Packers had 
acquired was still a new experience. 
Through junior high and early high 
school, the Packers were something 
less than a dominant force. But, of 
course, then Vince Lombardi arrived 


34 


2 ac te 


The crowd, and note the difference in dress (earflaps?) cheers madly as Packer 
Defense ends Giant scoring threat. No. 27 is John Symank. 


on the scene and changed all that. 

So here it was, late 1961 and it 
seemed likely that Green Bay would 
repeat as Western Division champion. 
Then there was that game with the 
Giants in Milwaukee, that 20-17 win 
which clinched the division crown. It 
took a fourth quarter come-from- 
behind luuchdown and a monumental 
(189-yard) performance from Jimmy 
Taylor, but a rematch with those 
Giants was a reality, 

But, this time, it would be for 
everything, and it would be in Green 
Bay. 

That was a bittersweet thing, the 
fact that the Packers would play host 
to this championship showdown. It 
meant that more than 39,000 fans 
would be able to see this history- 
making clash in person. I was happy 
for them because they had earned that 
right. 

But for me, it meant no television 
since Wausau fell into the blackout 
radius at that time. 


It only fleetingly crossed my mind to 
try and get a ticket. An askance look 
from my dad took care of that. We 
would, the look said, content 
ourselves with Ted Moore’s resonant 
radio voice, just as we had for all of 
Green Bay’s City Stadium games that 
year. 

Tickets in our town at that time 
were for the mayor, a couple of 
doctors and dentists and perhaps 
several other upper-crust types, such 
as tavern owners. They were not for 
high school kids, or former football 
coaches who could appreciate 
Lombardi’s brand of football better 
than nearly anyone else. 

While I merely appreciated the 
Packers’ excellence at that time, my 
father was a bit in awe of it. As a 
former coach, he was well aware of 
how rarely the game was played that 
well, that consistently. 

The weather during the week 
leading up to the game was a prime 
topic of conversation. While it 
warmed to 20 degrees (above zero) by 
gametime on Sunday, the mercury 
plunged to 10 below for much of the 
preceding week. The worry was thata 
frozen field would remove the 
effectiveness of Hornung and Taylor, 
or somehow deter Boyd Dowler or 
Max McGee from running their 
precise pass routes, or rob Bart Starr 
of his pinpoint accuracy. 

We had at that tirne, of course, no 
idea that this 1961 title game would be 
a balmy affair compared to the

The New New York, press recognized little Green Bay. 


legendary Ice Bowl against the Dallas 
Cowboys six years hence. We just 
knew it was a nasty week and I could 
still remember our frigid finale against 
Antigo nearly two months earlier and 
couldn't begin to understand how 
anyone could still be playing in the 
depths of a Wisconsin winter. 


The newspapers carried pictures of 
practices all week. There was a phozo 
of Hornung. Dowler, Ray and 
Nitschke—the four part-time soldiers 
that year—posing with Lombardi, all 
five of thom wearing stocking cape. 

There was the sobering news that 
Alex Webster and Roosevelt Brown 
were all healed up and ready to go, and 
that Jesse Whittenton and Bill 
Quinlan were down with severe colds 
and looked doubtful. 

Finally, it was Sunday and the stage 
was set. We took our seats at the 
kitchen table where the radio was at 
high volume. The first quarter was 
basically uneventful, as are first 
quarters of many championship 
games. But the second quarter, ah, 
that second quarter: 

Hornung for six yards and a 
touchdown ... Starr to Dowler for 13 
yards and a touchdown ... Starr to 
Ron Kramer for 14 yards and a 
touchdown ... Hornung, 17-yard field 
goal with no time left on the clock. 
Packers 24, Giants 0. Not much time 
left for the Giants, either. 

It was an unbelievable 15 minutes of 
football. It set 39,000 fans wild in City 
Stadium, their earflaps (remember 
them?) flying as they yelled 
themselves hoarse. And in kitchens 
and living rooms across Wisconsin, 
the cheers of fans released from long 
years of bondage were equally 
unrestrained. 


The Giants really had no zest for 
combat, it seemed, after that second 
quarter. Neither Conerly nor Tittle 
could light a fire under New York’s 
offense as Coach Allie Sherman 
chafed on the sidelines and club 
President John Mara silently watched 
the debacle from his press box seat 
above. 

Another Hornung field goal ... 
Starr to Kramer for 13 yards and a 
touchdown ... still another Hornung 
field goal, giving hima record 19 points 
and assuring him of the new Corvette 
awarded by Sport Magazine. 

And through it all, a defense that 
gave New York a mere 130 yards net. 

In the end, Lombardi pulled out 
Hornung and Taylor and Dowler and 
Starr one by one, allowing each his 
own ovation. 

The newspaper accounts of the 
game were read and re-read. But the 
one memory that has never dimmed 
with time is that famous picture of 
Lombardi riding off the field on the 
shoulders of Dan Currie and Dave 
Hanner, those piano keyboard teeth 
flashing in a huge grin. 

What followed that memorable day, 
of course, was nearly an entire decade 
of dominance. What followed, too, 
was growing up and becoming more 
sophisticated and more expectant of 
success. 

We had dared hope for a 
championship that year and we were 
rewarded with a 37-0 rout. 

It was only the beginning, but yet it 
was, in a sense, the end of our 
innocence. The championships that 
followed all had identities of their own, 
but none matched the wide-eyed 
appreciation we held of that first one. 

Twenty years... canitreally be that 
long ago?

DICKEY 
BREAKS 


SEVEN 
RECORDS 


Lynn Dickey returned to the wars 
with a bang in 1980. 

Plagued by injuries three of the last 
four years, Dickey took advantage of a 
new quick rhythm passing setup— 
plus the NFL’s new rules, and broke 
seven individual Packer passing 
records. 

It was the biggest single-season 
change in the passing records since 
Cecil Isbell went on a binge with Don 
Hutson in the early 1940s and 
established most of the original 
Packer aerial marks. Most of these 
were toppled by Tobin Rote and Bart 
Starr but now Dickey has established 
new marks. 

Dickey’s fireworks provided for 
four new team passing records. 

It was the passingest year in Packer 
history. A record 511 passes were 


= =“ f 
Lynn Dickey gets the media treatment after victory over Bengals. 


thrown to break the mark of 478 set 
back in 1951. The Bays gained 3,291 
yards passing to break the old record 
of 2,846, also set in 1951. The 
completion total of 289 broke the 
record of 240 set in 1979. The passing 
yards boosted the total yards gained 
figure to 5,087, which broke the 4,791- 
yard figure set by the championship 
1962 team. 

Dickey’s passing broke three of 
Starr’s records, two held by Rote and 
one each by Don Horn and John Hadl. 
Dickey broke some of them by a wide 
margin, although it must be 


The 
Record Book 


remembered he worked a 16-game 
schedule compared to 12 for Rote and 
14 for each of the others. Starr’s first 
five years were ina 12-game schedule. 
Here are Dickey’s new records: 
Most Passes Attempted Season 
478 (old record 382 by Rote in 1954). 
Most Passes Attempted Game 51 
(old record 46 by Starr in 1958). 
Most Passes Completed Season 
278 (old record 191 by John Hadl in 
1975). 
Most Passes Completed Game 35 
(old record 26 by Starr in 1958). 
Most Passes Had Intercepted 
Season 25 (old record 24 by Rote in 
1950). 
Most Yards Passing Season 3,529 
(old record 2,438 by Starr in 1962). 
Most Yards Passing Game 418 (old

record 410 by Horn in 1969). 


Dickey’s three “game” records 
were set in the five-quarter 14-14 
overtime tie at Tampa Bay. Starr’s two 
records set in 1958 were in a losing 
cause against the then-champion 
Baltimore Colts. 

The passing put two other Packers 
among the leaders. James Lofton 
nailed 71 passes in 1980 to just miss 
Don Hutson’s season record of 74 set 
in 1942. Eddie Lee Ivery caught 11 
passes against Tampa Bay to gaina tie 
for fourth with Bob Mann (1951) and 
Billy Howton (1954). The record of 14 
was set by Hutson vs. the New York 
Giants in '42, 

Two departed players left records, 
Kicker Chester Marcol set a record 
120 field goals in his nine-year career 
and Dave Beverly kicked a record 496 
punts in his six seasons. Marcol 
finished with 521 paints, fourth on the 
all-time list.

MIKE DOUGLASS: 


“When | Get A 
Check My Mom 
Gets a Check” 


By dill Lieber 
Milwaukee Sentinel 


Michael Douglass took his first trip 
to a better life when he was 10. He 
climbed aboard a Los Angeles city bus 
at 103rd St., in the ghetto of Watts, 
one of the largest and most depressing 
ghettos in the country, and he took a 
ride across town, away from the sun- 
baked asphalt and cement, away from 
the run-down buildings and shabby 
houses, as far from the crime-ridden 
streets as he could get. 

For 15 cents, he was carried outside 
the chain link fence that surrounded 
the ghetto, separating the bad life from 
the good. 

“Tt was like a dream,” said Douglass, 
who many years later grew up to bea 
star linebacker for the Packers. “It 
looked like something from a TV 
show. 

“Whenever I had saved up enough 
money — and there were times I had 
to scrape to buy an ice cream cone — 
I'd get on the bus and watch the cars 
go by. I'd say, ‘I want this car. I want 
that car. Someday, I’m going to have a 
life like this.’ 

“My mother impressed upon me 
early to get outside and explore ‘the 
other life.’ She’d say, ‘Go for a ride on 
the bus. See what’s out there.’ 

“She wanted to create a need — a 
force within me — to drive me to want 
a better life. So, I took her advice and! 
got out and looked.” 


After the bus rides, his life was 
never the same. 

Douglass grew up fighting the 
temptations and pressures of the 
streets in which he lived. And the 
battle to avoid being caught up in — 
and carried away by — the violence 
and sorrow was a never-ending one. 

But all the while, in the back of his 
mind, was the dream, the hope, the 
want that his mother had planted. 

“When I was little, my mother told 
me she was never going to come down 
and get me out of jail,” Douglass said. 
“And that’s something that stuck with 
me all the time | was growing up.” 

Douglass wore the shoes of the man 
of the house, and while his grade 
school and teenage friends were 
carrying guns and experimenting with 
drugs, he was carrying a load of 
responsibilities on his back. 

He has little recollection of his 
father, who deserted the family when 
Douglass was quite young. He and his 
three brothers and sisters were raised 
by his mother, Ollie. The family tried 
to make ends meet with a handful of 
jobs; Ollie worked almost around the 
clock as a domestic, and Douglass 
took on his second job at age 10, 
working for a janitorial service from 
9p.m. until 8a.m. 

In between jobs, the two of them 
took turns guiding the family — Ollie 
cooked, cleaned and sat her children 
down with books from the library; 
Douglass made sure his brothers and 


Mike Douglass — waiting for the next play. 


sisters went to school and stayed out 
of trouble. 

When there were free moments, 
like late at night when he was ready to 
go to his bed, Douglass sat back and 
looked upon his mother in awe. 
Mostly, though, he just listened to the 
sounds of the ghetto and he longed for 
the life he had seen from the bus 
windows. 

“Lots of times I couldn't understand 
how my mother kept going,” Douglass 
said. “I couldn’t see how she’d go from 
one job to another — and how she’d 
cook and clean on top of all that. 
There were times when she never 
went to sleep. I'd think, ‘How can a 
person be so driven?’ 

“But she loved us. Her drive pushed 
me, too. How could I just sit back and 
watch her? I couldn’t. There were alot 
of people around me — especially alot 
of my friends’ mothers — who sat 
around and picked up the welfare 
check. 

“My mother grew up in Mississippi 
She only made it through the sixth 
grade because she and her sister had 
to quit school and go to work. By 14, 
she was married. Life was hard in 
those days, and it started early. It’s 
those years of wisdom that she later 
passed on to me.” 

That wisdom, undying motivation 
and deep love for family, grew as 
Douglass matured into an adult. He 
was graduated from L.A. Jordan High 

Continued on Page 42 


a 


Continued from Page 41 
School in Watts with a 3.6 grade point 
average (on a 4-point scale). 

“T got only two Cs,” he said. “In my 
mind, and in my heart, I had a goal.” 

And although he hadn't taken up 
sports until he was in 10th grade, his 
outstanding performance merited a 
football scholarship to Arizona State. 
But after a year there, the 
responsibilities to his family pulled him 
home. He moved back to Watts and 
enrolled in Cal State — Los Angeles. 

“] wanted to get back into football, 
so I spent the next 24 years trying to 
get my grades up and get a 
scholarship,” he said. “J suffered; I 
made a lot of sacrifices. There were 
days I didn’t eat. 

“Later on, | moved to Long Beach 
and rode 2% hours every day to 
Hollywood. I studied on the bus. 

“There were times I'd call my 
mother and say, ‘I can't do this 
anymore. I want to quit and get ajob. I 
want to forget about football and 
come home.’ 

“She'd say, ‘I don’t believe you."” 

Douglass earned his associate 
degree and along with it, a scholarship 
to San Diego State. In 1978, he was 
drafted by the Packers in the fifth 
round. But even at the age of 26 and 
after three successful seasons in the 
NFL, don’t think Douglass still doesn’t 
dream. 

But he doesn’t dream of material 
things. He prides himself on being 
practical. 

“] always wanted a Mercedes,” he 
said, “But I bought myself a house in

ElCajun, Calif., instead. 

“When you don't have something, 
you want it. And then, when you can 
get it, you realize you don't really need 
if 

And, he doesn’t dream of traveling 
to faraway places. He still goes back to 
Watts to speak to students at his 
former high school. 

“It’s funny,” he said. “I don’t really fit 
in there anymore, I don't feel 
comfortable in Watts. I feel uneasy, 
paranoid. I see all the danger around 
me. I see my friends, who are either 
strung out on drugs or in jail — or 
dead. And, I wonder how | ever made 
it out alive,” 

Nor does he dream of being a 
superstar. There are those wha say at 
6 feet, 224 pounds, Douglass is too 
small to ever be a_ threatening 
linebacker in the NFL. Still, in his 
three seasons with Green Bay, he has 
been the most prominent and 
consistent performer on the Packer 
defense. 

“Football is not my whole life,” he 
said. “My family is the most important 
thing to me. It always has been and 
always will be. Some guys don’t think 
that way, and when football is out of 
their lives, they have nothing.” 

Instead, Douglass dreams the 
dreams of a child—the dreams of a 
better life, one filled with love, hope 
and optintism. 

“When I get a check, my mom gets a 
check,” said Douglass, whose mother 
moved to Oakland a few years ago. “I 
want my mother’s life to be 
comfortable. Now, it’s time for me to 
help her dream. I call her before |

“Coming through the project, | 
always thought, ‘I'm not going to let 
my mother suffer.’ Quite a few kids 
say that, but they find a way out, and 
they leave their parents behind. 

“A lot of people in that environment 
search for the easiest way out—the 
easiest way to make fast money. But! 
knew if I put forth the effort—and had 
a little patience—if I waited, it would 
come. 

“Tve been to the point where even 
though things weren't good, if | had 
anything to give up, I'd give up half. | 
was taught to share—no matter what 
the cost. My mother wouldn't let us 
kids fuss and holler; she'd tell us to 
divide it. 

“The same things apply to football. 1 
try to help everybody as much as | 
can. I'm not content to let somebody 
worry about his own position, I'll help 
out if he’s having trouble. 

“Professional football players 
should be able to relate the game to 
everyday life. If they can’t, then they'll 
never be winners, 

“A lot of guys come into pro ball 
thinking they are dedicated and 
disciplined, but you can tell which 
players had life easy by how they react 
when things get hard. They don't 
know how to make sacrifices. They 
don’t know how to reach deeply inside 
themselves. At no point can they dig 
down and say, ‘I really want it.” 

“T've got that drive to sustain. [don’t 
feel there's anything I can’t achieve if I 
set my mind to it.”

A SIGNIFICANT ITEM 


With a 5-10-1 finish, the Packers 
didn’t have a whole lot to cheer about 
in 1980 except maybe one 
significant item. 

The team that won the Central 
Division championship, the 
Minnesota Vikings, was beaten twice 
by Green Bay, 16-3 at home and 25-13 
at Bloomington. The Vikings finished 
witha 9-7 record, tied with Detroit, but 
Minnesota won it on points. The Vikes 
and Lions split. 

The Packers scored their other 
victories over the Bears, Bengals and 
49ers. Ironically, the Bengals downed 


the world champion Steelers twice 
while the Pack lost aclosely contested 
22-20 decision to the Steels. 

Green Bay played two overtime 
games in 1980. The first was in the 
opener, a 12-6 verdict over the Bears 
on a touchdown by Kicker Chester 
Marcol who picked off a blocked field 
goal attempt and then ran it in. 

The other overtime was a full five- 
quarter 14-all knot with the 
Buccaneers in Tampa Bay. Green Bay 
rolled up a record 569 yards but two 
missed field goals by Tom Birney 
ruined a victory chance. 


The Vikings, who went on to the 
playoffs and then lost to Super Bowl 
finalist Philadelphia, was nicked for 
773 yards by the Packers in the two 
games. The total included 393 yards 
by rushing, with 247 coming in the 
second game when Eddie Lee Ivery 
and Gerry Ellis each gained over 100 
yards. 

The Packers had two extremely low 
spots during the season. The Rams 
got the jump on the Pack in Game 3 
and roared off toa51-7 lead before the 


Continued on Page 44 
43 


Continued from Page 43 


Packers scored twice in the fourth 
quarter. 

The Bays slammed back from that 
low to post 4-4-1 in the next nine 
games before being eliminated from 
title contention by the Bucs in 
Milwaukee 20-17. The Packers went 
into Chicago for Game 14 the next 
Sunday quite flat and succumbed 61-7 
to the eager Bears.

James Lofton relaxes on bench while defense takes over. 


By Mike Christopulos 
Milwaukee Sentinel 


A Green Bay newspaper ran a car- 
toon about James Lofton during the 
1979 season. It called him, among 
other things, a “ham-handed and over- 
paid receiver.” 

That unflattering cartoon made a 
deep impression on the Green Bay 
Packers’ gifted wide receiver. 

“It kind of went in line with 
everything else that was being written 
about me,” Lofton reflected. “Things 
kind of went sour because people felt I 
wasn’t playing as well as I could have. 

“I set out to do some things 
personally last year and | did those. | 
had some statistical goals. But even 
more than that some people had lost 
faith in my ability to perform at a high 
level and had questioned how good I 
really was. I wanted to set all that 
straight.” 

Lofton silenced his critics by 
catching 71 passes for 1,226 yards. 
That virtuoso performance not only 
earned Lofton all-pro honors, but put 


his name into the record book 
alongside such ex-Packer greats as 
Don Hutson and Bill Howton. 

It all added up to Lofton’s most 
satisfying season as a pro and helped 
make up for all the trials and 
tribulations he had to endure in 1979. 

Lofton was in hot water almost 
everytime he turned around in 1979. 

First, there was an incident after the 
team’s 27-21 overtime loss to the 
Minnesota Vikings. Lofton came into 
the locker room during the postgame 
prayer, angrily threw his shoulder 
pads into a locker and had words with 
Coach Bart Starr. 

Then, during the Packers’ 27-22 loss 
to the New York Jets at Lambeau 
Field, he made an obscene gesture at 
fans after they booed him for fumbling 
away a completed pass. Later, during 
a taped television interview, he used 
obscenities in discussing what 
happened. 

After that incident, Chuck Lane, 
who was then the Packers’ public 
relations director, called Lofton, “a 
prima donna who needs to grow up.” 


Lofton 
Silences 
Critics 


Predictably, Lofton started 
receiving some “hate” mailin the wake 
of his troubles. “Over the last eight 
games of that season | probably got 
over 500 letters and maybe about 20- 
25 were negative,” said Lofton. 


Lofton says now he’d react 
differently to similar adverse 
incidents. 


“There are always things you can 
get mad about,” said Lofton. “I try to 
disassociate myself with the pivotal 
ups and downs. I just go out and play 
my game. After the game’s over, that’s 
the end of it.” 

“| think a lot of changes happened in 
his life this past year,” said his wife 
Beverly. “I'm sure you would never 
see him cuss on television again. He’s 
told me he doesn’t get as angry as he 
used to.” 

Lofton met his wife at a New Year’s 
Eve party in 1979 and they were 
married in Hawaii last January while 
Lofton was there for a Pro Bowl 
appearance. 

Getting married was one of the best 

Continued on Page 48 


47 


Continued fram Page 47 


things that ever happened to Lofton. 

“It helped a lot,” said Lofton. “It 
gave me somebody to depend on, it 
made my life a little more complete.” 

His wife, who has a_ speech 
pathology degree from Ouachita 
College in Arkadelphia, Ark., has had 
a profound influence on Lofton, 

When the two met, Beverly thought 
Lofton was a student at Stanford. He 
came across as a very quiet, innocent 
and shy person. 


“Finally after we started seeing each 
other regularly, this egocentric quy 
came out,” Beverly said. “I thought he 
was a little bit unto himself and I told 
him that | thought too many people 
put him up on a pedestal and made 
him seem like a God. I don’t believe in 
putting people up on pedestals. 

“I tore him down sometimes,” she 
continued. “I told him he was no better 
than anybody else and had a job just 
like everybody else. 

“Basically, he believed that too. But 
he had to put on this facade in order to 
defend himself. 

“T told him he didn’t have to do that 
to make me happy. He didn’t have to 
be Mr. Big Stuff. He knew he could 
just be Jarnes and | would understand. 

“Sometimes, he would hurt my 
feelings because I'm one of those 
people who tries to sugar-coat 
everything. We had to straighten that 
out. [ could tell he was a good person 
the first time I met him. I knew all he 
was doing was running people away 
from him, Success didn’t mean he had 
to be that way.” 

His wife refuses to take credit for 
the way Lofton has changed—for the 
better—in his dealings with other 
people. 

“James started going to chapel and 
he realized that a lot of things he'd 
done were not Christian-like” said 
Beverly. 

“He realized there was a better way 
to go. Just before we were married, he 
announced he wanted to have a 
Christian marriage and wanted to put 
God first, me second and his job and 
everything else in its place following 
that.” 

Even though Lofton has become 
more serious, there's still a lot of the 
little boy in him. And he hasn't lost his 
weird sense of humor. 

His wife can vouch for that. They 
had been shopping for nearly five 
hours one day earlier this winter in 
San Jose, Calif. 

They happened to be at a Macy’s

getting a bit antsy and weary of the 
shopping ordeal. Suddenly, he 
plopped himself down on the middle of 
the floor like a child throwing a 
tantrum. 

“He didn’t kick or throw a tantrum,” 
Beverly recalled. “It was more like a 
fainting spell. But it was terrible 
because here’s this big, 6 foot 3 guy on 
the floor. It was just his way of getting 
me out of the store.” 

Packer receiver coach Lew 
Carpenter considers Lofton and San 
Diego's James Jefferson the two finest 
receivers in the NFL. And Lofton 
agrees. 

“T look at my physical attributes, 
speed, agility, hands and size,” said 
Lofton, “I’m just as good as any other 
receiver or better.” 

Lofton feels he’s a much better 
receiver now than when he first broke 
into the NFL in 1978. 

“It's hard to hang a percentage on 
how much better I am,” said Lofton. 
“But I know the game a lot better. 
Physically, I've gotten better, I'm a 


little bigger and a little faster. For 
sheer knowledge, I know maybe twice 
as much now as | did when I first 
started playing.” 

Green Bay’s wide receiving talent— 
Aundra Thompson, Fred Nixon, Ron 
Cassidy and Bobby Kimball—drops 
off quite a bit after Lofton. 

This has worked to Lofton's 
disadvantage because he invariably 
faces double coverage. 

“Tve learned to work around it,” 
said Lofton. “Most of the passes I 
catch are in the flow of the game and 
get us first downs. But I think if 1 was 
single covered a little more, I'd had 
more opportunities fo score.” 

Like so many other people Lofton 
was surprised when the Packers made 
quarterback Rich Campbell of 
California their No. 1 draft choice last 
April. 

“Hopefully, it'll help our team 
sometime,” said Lofton. “It’s hard to 
say whether he'll have an immediate 
impact. I've heard that he’s big and 
strong and a good passer.” 

Lofton was saluted as the Packers’ 
offensive player of the year at the 
Wisconsin Pro Football Awards 
dinner held in Milwaukee last 
February. 

At that affair, Lofton was asked 
about rumors of a possible trade 
sending him to the Oakland Raiders 
for quarterback Dan Pastorini, 

“When I heard it, it was too good to 
be true,” said Lofton. 

“Basically, | was making that 
comment in jest,” Lofton said, “I’m 
from the West Coast and I'd like to 
play there one day. But I'm happy 
playing with the Packers right now.” 

And the Packers are happy to have 
him, considering all the superlative 
things he did during the 1980 season.

SUPER SNOW BOWL 


The Super Bowl will be played ina 
snow city for the first time in 198Z and 
the National Football League had 
better pray that there isn’t a mid- 
winter blizzard in Pontiac, Mich., the 
weekend of Jan. 24. 

No, Super Bow! XVI won't be 
played outdoors. It will be contested 
inside the Lions’ home site, the 
Silverdome. But what happens if a 
huge blizzard keeps people, including 
the contestants, from reaching the 
“dome” on the appointed day? Banish 
the thought! 

Seriously, you can bet the NFL is 
making serious snowplow prepara- 
tions—just in case the Detroit area 
doesn't have a third-straight mild 
winter. 

The Super Bowl has been played 
five times in both Miami and New 
Orleans. Los Angeles’ Coliseum and 
the Pasadena Rose Bowl hosted two 
games each. One game was played in 
Houston. 

Other than a sprinkle or two in 
Miami, weather has never been a 
factor in the Super Bowl. And it won't 
be in 1982—at least inside the 
Silverdome. This will be the third 
indoor game, the other two being held 
in New Orleans’ Superdome in 1978 
and ’81.

Divisional Playoffs (CBS and NBC); Sunday, Jan. 3—NFC and AFC Divisional Playoffs (CBS and NBC); Sunday,

63 


Packer Schedule 
Different, Challenging 


Ah... the schedule. 

Every year it’s different, bloodier 
and strewn with pitfalls. 

It's the route you take to the 
Playoffs and then, hopefully, the 
Super Bowl. 

The Packers are supposed to have 
an easier schedule this season, based 
on last year’s record, but don’t tell that 
to Coach Bart Starr, who explains it 
this way: 

“People who get trapped into that 
kind of thinking pay the price later.” 

The Packers meet playoff teams in 
three of their first four games and then 
play four of their last five games on the 
road. Starr feels “it’s a little 
disadvantage to be playing that many 
games on the road at the end.”. 


The Packer Schedule 


{All Wisconsin Times) 
PRE-SEASON 

Sat., Aug, 8—Cowboys at Dallas, 8:00 

Sat,, Aug. 15—Raiders at Milwaukee, 7,00. 

Sat,, Aug. 22—Broncos at Denver, 7:30 

Sal, Aug. 29—Browns at Green Bay, 7:00. 


LEAGUE 
Sun., Sept. 6—Bears at Chicago, 1:00 

Sun, Sept. 13—Falcons at Green Bay 12:00 
Sun, Sept. 20—Rams al Los Angeles, 3:00, 
Sun,, Sept. 27—Vikings at Milwaukee, 1:00. 
Sun, Oct. 4 Giants at New York, 12:00, 
Sun, Oct. 11—Bucs al Green Bay, 1:00 
Sun, Oct. 18—49ers at Milwaukee, 12:00 
Sun, Oct, 25—Lions at Detroit, 12:00. 

Sun., Nov 1—Seahawks at Green Bay, 1:00. 
Sun. Nov 8—Giants at Milwaukee, 12:00 
Sun,, Nov, 15—Bears at Green Bay, 12.00 
Sun, Nov, 22—Bucs at Tampa Bay, 12:00. 
Sun, Nov, 29—Vikings at Minnesota. 12:00 
Sun, Dec. 6—Lions at Green Bay, 12:00. 
Sun, Dec. 13—Saints at New Orleans, 12:00 
Sun,, Dec. 20—Jets at New York, 12.00. 


But, as Bart put it, “we get the 
schedule and we play it. | think it’s a 
great challenge. And I know our 
people will look at it the same way.” 

Figured on their 5-10-1 finish of a 
year ago, the Packers have a different 
type of schedule this season. 

They have 11 different opponents 
instead of the 12 they had since the 16- 
game schedule was started in 1978. 

This means the Packers have a 
home-and-home set with an additional 
team besides the four against Central 
Division teams. The added two-game 
sawoff is against New York Giants. 
Thus, the Packers play 10 games 
involving the Giants and Division foes 
Chicago, Detroit, Minnesota and 
Tampa Bay. 

The remaining six games are 
against Seattle and the New York Jets 
of the AFC and the four teams in the 
NFC’s Western Divisions, Los 
Angeles, Atlanta, New Orleans and 
San Francisco. 

The Packers get a good chance to 
sharpen their claws with a tough pre- 
season schedule, meeting the 
Cowboys, Super Bowl champion 
Raiders, Broncos and Browns in that 
order. 

The Bays open against the Bears for 
the third straight season, this time in 
Chicago. The two rivals split the last 
two openers but the Packers can 
hardly forget their last visit to 
Chicago, resulting in a 61-7 walloping 
Dec. 7. This was quite far removed 
from the Pack’s 12-6 overtime victory 
over the Bears in Green Bay Sept. 7.
